### On
## the Ancient and Modern
# Mysteries. <term>Mysteries</term> <note>In the 18th century, "Mysteries" referred to secret religious rites. This often included both the ancient Greek traditions, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, and the contemporary secret societies like the Freemasons which were popular during the Enlightenment.</note>

## Preface.

Before my readers engage with this small treatise itself, I owe them a preliminary explanation regarding the occasion for its writing. Since so many writings of this kind are appearing in the world in our times, and the various ways of perceiving <note>original: "Vorstellungsarten," referring to different schools of thought or conceptual frameworks</note> them are so numerous, it initially seemed quite superfluous to me to increase their number further with my own. However, the request of one of my friends soon brought me back from this opinion. I was

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, detailing his initial struggle with the source material and his decision to move beyond a simple translation.</meta>

namely, I was requested by the same individual to translate the *Research on Ancient and Modern Initiations* <note>original: "Recherches sur les Initiations anciennes et modernes"</note>, which was published in Paris in 1779 by Abbot R. <note>Likely referring to Abbé Robin, a French historian of secret societies.</note>; and I could not do otherwise than gladly comply with this request. I had not yet translated much when I already realized that I had undertaken a very thankless task. I found much of the material presented so superficially and so incorrectly that I soon deemed it necessary to correct the author's errors through my own annotations. However, the further I progressed, the more I finally recognized the impossibility of this undertaking. The author had already overloaded his book with notes as it was; if mine were now to be added as well, it would have eventually become a collection of notes without any text, and the lit—

<page-num>III</page-num>

...portion of the text did not seem to me to be worth the effort of wasting so much time upon it.

In the other part of the work, where the author deals with the initiations <term>Einweihungen: formal rites of passage into secret societies</term> of more recent times, and specifically with **Masonry** <note>original: "Maurerey"</note>, I found even more to address. Upon some reflection, it could not long remain hidden from me that the author views the entire matter from a very incorrect point of view. He must either have never belonged to this society of which he speaks—and in that case, what can be said about it with any thoroughness?—or he must be devoted to certain prejudices which, according to my convictions, not only cannot be brought into harmony <note>original: "Accord"</note> with the truth of the Order, but also certainly do not show it in a favorable light.

# Preface

...to set before the eyes of the world. Here, it was quite impossible to come to the aid of the book through corrective notes.

I therefore decided to rather abandon the entire translation <note>original: "Uebersezung"</note> and to fulfill the promise once given to my friend through an original work on this subject. This is the occasion for this small treatise <note>original: "Abhandlung"</note>.

One will soon realize, without my reminding, that I have not written for strangers, nor for the great scholarly world, but solely for members of the Society <note>original: "Gesellschaft"</note> known by the name of **Freemasons**. Even though I have often had to take refuge in the sources of external erudition <note>original: "Gelehrsamkeit"</note>; nevertheless, it is not those scholars, but only these members who can accurately and

I have therefore sought, as much as I was able, to provide such correct concepts of true Freemasonry <note>original: "Maurerey"; the author distinguishes between "true" masonry and the various fringe or "irregular" groups common in the 18th century</note> as I could give without infringing upon my duties <note>a reference to the Masonic oath of secrecy regarding specific rituals and signs</note>. Should these efforts not please everyone—where duty, philanthropy, and brotherly love speak, there all other objections must remain silent.

## Note.

Despite the most careful efforts to provide an error-free book, a few small mistakes have nonetheless occurred during printing.
page 87. line 9. it should read: holy chests <note>original: "Laden"; likely referring to the Ark of the Covenant or ritual containers</note>, instead of lands.
In the same place, in the 16th line: *Secret Discipline* <note>original Latin: "Disciplina Arcani"; the practice of hiding certain theological tenets from non-believers</note>, instead of *Disciplinu Arcani*.
page 144. second line: Means, instead of Meuns.
page 167. in the last line: Teachings, instead of Teaching.
page 194. tenth line: Mr. Court Councilor Heine <note>A title for a high-ranking advisor to a sovereign</note>, instead of Hinne.
page 380. second line from the end: *udam* <note>original: "udam"; likely a correction to a Latin or Sanskrit term</note>, instead of *vedam*.

## I.

# On the Origin of the Mysteries.

---

The origin of the Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refer to secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were restricted to initiated members.</note>, which enjoyed such extraordinary prestige throughout the entire ancient world, belongs to those problems that can hardly be determined with complete certainty. What has been said about them until now, and what can still be said, are merely conjectures <note>original: "Conjecturen"; scholarly guesses or hypotheses</note>, among which one may always possess more probability than another, yet not a single one provides sufficient certainty. What one finds regarding this in the writings of the Ancients—which are, after all, the only sources from which one can draw—clarifies these obscurities just as little. These are merely accounts of the various transmissions <note>original: "Translationen"; the movement or carrying over of traditions</note> of these rites to other nations. It is said that Cadmus <note>The legendary Phoenician prince and founder of Thebes</note> and Inachus <note>The mythological first king of Argos</note> are supposed to have [brought] them

<summary>This introductory page explores the historical difficulty of identifying the true origins of ancient secret religious mysteries, noting that primary sources only record how they were passed between cultures rather than how they began.</summary>
<keywords>Ancient Mysteries, religious origins, historiography, Cadmus, Inachus, Greek mythology, transmission of culture</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the spread of ancient wisdom; this page continues the list of legendary figures who brought these traditions to Greece.</meta>

to Greece, Erechtheus <note>A legendary archaic king of Athens, often associated with the founding of the Panathenaic Games.</note> to Athens, Trophonius to Boeotia, Melampus to Argos, Minos to Crete, Orpheus to Thrace, and others have brought them elsewhere.

These reports certainly indicate to which persons the introduction of the **mysteries** <note>The "Mysteries" refer to secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were intended only for initiated members.</note> in this or that land is to be attributed; but the actual origin of the same remains just as obscure as it was. And how certain are these reports even now? Perhaps nothing definite and perfectly certain can ever be said about this. Perhaps the reports of the origin of the mysteries themselves already belonged to those things that were not intended for the public, and about which only those who were perfectly instructed received information. This seems to me to have a great deal of probability. Not only things of greater significance, not only higher **philosophy**, or **theology**, or whatever else one might wish to call it, constituted the subject of the secrets of the ancients; **history** likewise belonged here—

<summary>This page discusses the legendary figures credited with spreading mystery traditions throughout Greece and argues that the true origins of these rites were likely kept secret even from the general public.</summary>
<keywords>Erechtheus, Trophonius, Melampus, Minos, Orpheus, ancient mysteries, secret societies, Greek mythology, historiography</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 2, the author is discussing the necessity of discretion regarding historical details that might seem trivial but are linked to deeper institutional secrets.</meta>

Often, even a single historical circumstance, which one might otherwise consider a secondary matter or a trifle, sheds light on other things; and if one does not wish to discover and make these public, one is placed under the necessity of concealing the former as well.

True members of our <term>Society<note>German: "Gesellschaft." Likely referring to the Freemasons or a similar initiatory order.</note></term> can judge this most correctly, as our own experience is close at hand. If one wished to place the object of our secrets in any single historical circumstance, one would be very mistaken. Our history, considered in itself, is no secret. It becomes one, however, because other things concerning this side of the world would be discovered that do not belong in the public eye; and therefore, the question regarding the origin of our <term>Order<note>German: "Ordens." A formal organization, often with religious or fraternal connotations.</note></term> and its secrets has likewise become a matter that is just as carefully hidden from all strangers as that which the secrets themselves contain. Perhaps it was the same case with the <term>Mysteries<note>German: "Mysterien." Referring to the ancient Greco-Roman mystery cults, such as those of Eleusis.</note></term> of the ancient world.

<vocab>Society, Secrets, Order, Mysteries, Circumstances</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that the history of their secret society is kept hidden not because the history itself is scandalous, but because it serves as a gateway to deeper secrets that must remain private. He draws a parallel between his contemporary Order and the ancient Mystery cults of antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>secret societies, historical concealment, institutional origins, ancient mysteries, discretion, fraternal orders</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "Einige" (Some), leading into this discussion on the origins of ancient mysteries.</meta>

Furthermore, one finds that almost universally, the mysteries of the ancient world were sacred to certain deities, from whom their origins were also derived. Thus, the Egyptians traced their mysteries back to Osiris and Isis. The Eleusinian mysteries <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually for the cult of Demeter and Persephone.</note> were communicated to the Athenians by Ceres <note>The Roman name for Demeter, goddess of agriculture.</note>, to whom they were sacred. The orgies <note>In this historical context, "orgies" refers to the ecstatic religious rituals or festivals of Dionysus, rather than the modern connotation.</note> dedicated to Bacchus <note>Also known as Dionysus, the god of wine and revelry.</note> likewise had him as their founder and author. Perhaps these were nothing other than hieroglyphs <note>Used here metaphorically to mean "sacred symbols" or "encoded meanings."</note> under which the true origin was concealed—a truth made known only to those to whom all images were fully explained. What the Greeks say regarding the founding of their Eleusinian mysteries by Ceres immediately resembles a priest’s tale or a legend <gloss>sacred story <note>original: "ἱερὸς λόγος" (hieros logos)</note></gloss> so clearly that it is impossible to mistake it. For the common people, such narratives were sufficient. However, this is not the first time that matters of greater importance have been hidden beneath such sacred myths.

<summary>This page discusses how ancient mystery cults attributed their origins to specific deities like Osiris, Isis, and Ceres, suggesting these myths served as symbolic "hieroglyphs" to hide deeper truths from the uninitiated.</summary>
<keywords>Ancient Mysteries, Egyptian Mythology, Eleusinian Mysteries, Osiris, Isis, Ceres, Bacchus, Hieros Logos, Religious Symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
Some more recent scholars have attempted to derive the **mysteries** <note>The "mysteries" refer to the secret religious rites of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which involved initiation and hidden knowledge.</note> of ancient peoples from the ages of **savagery**. In America, even to this day among the wild tribes, there exists a kind of mystery consisting of a certain religious **bogeyman** <note>original: "Popanz" – a frightening figure or bugbear used to intimidate the uninitiated.</note> and **hocus-pocus**, which are in the hands of the **jugglers** <note>original: "Jongleurs" – here used in the historical sense of medicine men, shamans, or ritual performers.</note> and take place when another is to be admitted into the number of the jugglers. 

But one need only be somewhat acquainted with the mysteries of today’s savages and those of the ancient world to be easily convinced that the latter cannot be placed in any parallel with the former; they are of such a nature that they can be reckoned from nothing less than the ages of savagery. Even if they likewise had their bogeyman in some respects, they nevertheless also had many useful, impressive, and—if one analyzed the **symbolism**—highly important ceremonies, which cannot be compared at all with the empty **mummeries** <note>original: "Gaukeleyen" – deceptive tricks, jugglery, or frivolous performances.</note> that are customary among the savages.

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: can.</meta>

<summary>The author argues against the theory that ancient religious mysteries originated from primitive savagery, contrasting the sophisticated symbolism of ancient rites with the "hocus-pocus" of contemporary indigenous shamans in America.</summary>
<keywords>ancient mysteries, religious history, indigenous rituals, symbolism, initiation, cultural evolution, shamanism</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>The catchword "werden" from the previous page connects to the opening verb of this page, completing the thought regarding the distinction of these societies.</meta>

can. The most distinguished feature, however, which separates them from the others, is the scientific element, which was found throughout the mysteries of the ancients, and of which I shall have the opportunity to speak more extensively hereafter. This is something that does not belong to such times when peoples were still in a state of lack of culture <note>original: "Unkultur"; referring to a primitive or undeveloped societal state</note>, but naturally presupposes a fairly high degree of cultivation. And that was the essential and primary character of the secrets <note>The author uses "Geheimnisse" (secrets) interchangeably with "Mysterien" (mysteries)</note> which stood in such high regard in the ancient world.

Warburton <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop and author of "The Divine Legation of Moses"</note>, who has otherwise incorporated a very beautiful treatise on the mysteries into his *Divine Legation of Moses*, derives their origin from the legislators, by whom, in his opinion, they were invented, arranged, and supported. It can certainly be denied by no one that the authorities <term>Obrigkeit: the governing body or civil magistrates</term> knew of the interior workings of the secrets, and that they also stood under their special supervision and protection. A society that also dealt with things that were concealed from the authorities

<summary>The author argues that ancient mysteries were defined by their scientific nature and high level of cultural development, while critiquing Bishop Warburton's theory that these mysteries were purely inventions of the state.</summary>
<keywords>Ancient Mysteries, William Warburton, Divine Legation of Moses, civil authority, scientific knowledge, cultural development, secret societies</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 6: The author continues the critique of William Warburton's theories regarding the origins of ancient mystery cults.</meta>

had to be, deserves nothing less than tolerance. What is more, the mysteries of the ancient world were also, in many respects, closely connected with the state, and the authorities therefore had to take a precise interest in them. But for that reason, nothing is more incorrect than **Warburton's** <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop and social theorist who argued in 'The Divine Legation of Moses' that the mysteries were state-invented tools for social control.</note> idea that the secrets of the ancient world were invented by legislators in order to preserve, in such a manner, the great truths of the **immortality of the human soul**, and of punishments and rewards.

The reasons with which he supports his opinion are certainly not sufficient to prove this. He first cites that the mysteries had their origin in Egypt, and appeals to the testimony of **Herodotus** and **Diodorus of Sicily**. It is indeed true that the Greek mysteries, and especially the **Eleusinian mysteries** <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually in honor of Demeter and Persephone.</note>, took their origin from Egypt, and only underwent certain transformations according to the taste of the Greeks, and applications

Perhaps the Thracian, Cretan, and Phoenician mysteries were drawn from this very same source. It is also true that the Egyptian priests were the actual, authentic interpreters of the state laws, just as the priests were in the state of the Jews, which was established according to the Egyptian model. Greece and Britain, all of whom were kings and legislators, such as Zoroaster, Inachus, Orpheus, Melampus, Trophonius, Minos, Cinyras, Erechtheus, and the Druids. — Regarding some of those cited here, their status as king and legislator is not yet established. Cinyras combined the priesthood with the royal dignity in his person, and the question there is whether he introduced the mysteries to Cyprus as a priest or as a king? Melampus was not a king, but a soothsayer. The same applies to Orpheus, Trophonius, and the Druids. But even assuming this were the case, it proves nothing more than that the mysteries of the ancient world, wherever they were established, always stood under the protection of the authorities and the laws from their first introduction; it does not prove, however, that kings and legislators were the inventors of them.

Warburton’s final reason is this: that the state presided over the secrets <note>William Warburton (1698–1779) was an English bishop who argued in "The Divine Legation of Moses" that ancient mystery cults were state-run tools to teach morality and the afterlife.</note>. — This applied, at most, only to the **Eleusinian Mysteries** <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually for the cult of Demeter and Persephone.</note>. Everything that can be said with certainty regarding the introduction of the mysteries among ancient peoples is this: that from the beginning, they stood under the protection of the laws. Furthermore, what Warburton cites for his hypothesis—based on the characteristics of the mysteries (since no slave or foreigner was permitted to join), the necessity of a virtuous life, and the beneficial influences they had on the state—only proves their value. I must here, right at the outset, touch upon the main subject of the *secrets* <note>original: "Geheimnisse"; refers here to the hidden knowledge of ancient initiatory societies</note> of the ancient world, and their essential character is the assertion of certain doctrines that contrasted with the prevailing religion to which the common man adhered. The prevailing religion of all those peoples among whom *mysteries* <note>original: "Mysterien"; specifically the Greco-Roman and Egyptian mystery cults like those at Eleusis</note> existed—the *Egyptians*, the *Greeks*—was *polytheism* <note>original: "Vielgötterey"; the worship of many gods</note>. In the *mysteries*, a completely different religion, or philosophy, or whatever else one wishes to call it, was presented. The original religion of the ancient world was not *polytheism*, but *deism* <note>The author uses "Deismus" here to suggest a primitive belief in a single supreme being or a rational creator, rather than the complex pantheons of later antiquity</note>. *Polytheism* was a degeneration of the same, which arose partly through improper allegorical instruction concerning God and His attributes, partly through an incorrect conception of the world of spirits and bodies, and partly through the dispersal of nations, and

<summary>The author argues that ancient mystery cults were established to preserve an original monotheistic or deistic faith that stood in opposition to the popular polytheism of the masses. He suggests that the worship of many gods was a later corruption caused by misunderstood allegories and the physical dispersal of peoples.</summary>

<keywords>mysteries, polytheism, deism, ancient religion, Egypt, Greece, religious degeneration, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the word "and," leading into the discussion of the decline of ancient knowledge.</meta>

the penetration of barbarism <note>The author uses "barbarism" to describe the loss of classical learning and the rise of intellectual darkness.</note> associated with it, partly prompted by other circumstances, and nourished and raised by the self-interest of the priesthood, until finally the originally true religion was completely displaced. In these early times, when this great revolution in the religious system of all peoples occurred—when one transitioned from deism <note>The belief in a single, rational creator based on reason rather than revelation.</note> to polytheism <note>The worship of many gods.</note>—one must, in all probability, seek the origin of the mysteries. To suppress this doctrine, which is so closely related to human understanding, and to surrender it to oblivion, seemed unjustifiable. To banish polytheism, however, was permitted neither by superstition, the prejudices of the people, nor many other circumstances and personal advantages. One therefore allowed the latter to take its course, while simultaneously preserving the former, yet only within the narrow boundaries of a consecrated priesthood. These priests were already generally regarded by the people as a far superior kind of human being, acting as the intermediaries between humanity and the deity.

It is easily seen that this origin, which I attribute to the mysteries <note>The "mysteries" refer to the secret religious rites of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were restricted to initiated members.</note>, does not apply to all nations among whom they took place. At least not to the Greeks, from whose mysteries the most information has been passed down to posterity. But it is also certain that the Greeks were not a people who had sunk from deism <note>The belief in a supreme being or creator who does not intervene in the universe.</note> to polytheism <note>The belief in or worship of more than one god.</note>, but rather a people who, over time, improved their crass polytheism with the help of philosophy. As these became known, the Greek deities were also gradually introduced into Egypt, partly by being brought in directly, and partly by assigning Greek names and myths to originally Egyptian deities. Consequently, a strange blending of Greek and Egyptian religion arose. If the Mysteries—which later passed over to the Greeks and were reshaped according to their taste and religion—were truly invented in Egypt, then this invention would likely be placed in the period that elapsed between Abraham and Joseph. This was the era when the rational service of the one true God <note>The author refers here to Monotheism or "Natural Religion."</note> was abandoned and polytheism <term>polytheism: the worship of many gods</term> was introduced in its place. To preserve that original belief nonetheless, it was enclosed within the inaccessible darkness of sacred secrets, which were open only to the priests alone.

### II.
## The Reputation of the Mysteries among the Ancients.

In these investigations, one cannot venture a single step without complaining everywhere about the lack of necessary information. However, this much can be concluded with reasonable certainty: that the reputation in which the Mysteries <term>Mysterien: secret religious rites of ancient Greece and Rome, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were restricted to initiated members</term> were held by the ancients was uncommonly great.

I will not deny that the mysterious darkness with which everything was shrouded played a fairly large part in this veneration. Even if it happens often enough that things one does not know are judged unfavorably

<summary>The author argues that despite a lack of historical records, the ancient Mysteries held immense social and religious prestige, partly due to the allure of their secrecy.</summary>
<keywords>Mysteries, ancient history, history of religion, secrecy, scholarship, veneration</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the concept of protection or "Schutz," leading into the discussion of how the mysteries were maintained and perceived by the public.</meta>

becoming so, it nevertheless depends only on a certain turn given to them, and thus they are the object of general esteem and admiration. The prudence of those in whose hands the mysteries <note>The "mysteries" refer to the secret religious rites of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian or Egyptian mysteries, which were hidden from the uninitiated.</note> were held had thus known how to arrange it so that, as the most sublime sanctuaries, they were held in great respect and prestige even by those to whom access to them was denied. Added to this was the fact that they were in the hands of the priesthood, who already enjoyed the favorable prejudice of a very special holiness and an intimate association with the deity. And even among the priests, not everyone had a share in them, but only the most distinguished, and those who had been found worthy after various trials. Among some peoples, namely the Egyptians, with whom the priesthood was bound to certain families, these families alone had exclusive access to the mysteries over the entire rest of the nation. Furthermore, there was the fact that they were especially under the

protection of religion, and were themselves regarded as the most sacred part of it. This they indeed were in fact. For the outward religion, to which the common people adhered, was the most foolish superstition that could be imagined, and the strangest mixture of all kinds of fables, which were often of such a nature that no beneficial impressions could be made upon morals from that source. What was presented, on the contrary, in the **Mysteries** <note>The "Mysteries" (Mysterien) refer to secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were open only to initiated members.</note>, was essentially the doctrine of sound reason, and in a certain respect one can even say, divine truth, which was accompanied by the most emphatic teachings and precepts for virtue. To this was finally added a certain outward element. Even if the interior remained closed to the people, certain outward ceremonies and processions were nevertheless not hidden from them, and these, as things belonging to the most sacred parts of religion, were generally associated with much splendor. The mysteries were veiled from all foreigners, and the priesthood, in whose hands they exclusively remained, fostered a general reverence for them through the idea that they were the most holy part of the entire religion; yet, the outward splendor certainly did no less to achieve this. Humans remain humans—that is, creatures accustomed to the sensory—and it is quite impossible that things which stir the senses in such a way should not simultaneously make an impression upon the heart.

But as great as the prestige was in which the mysteries <note>The term "mysteries" refers to the secret religious rites of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were restricted to initiated members.</note> stood among foreigners, it was equally great among those who were initiated into them. One can conclude this with reasonable certainty from the great caution with which they were hidden from all who did not belong to the priestly lineage. To be sure, the great contradiction in which the mysteries stood against the popular religion <note>The "popular religion" refers to the public, state-sanctioned polytheism practiced by the common people, as opposed to the philosophical or esoteric truths hidden in the mysteries.</note> likely played a large part in this concealment. The latter had taken such a hold of the minds of the people,

One would nevertheless be mistaken to believe that access to them was open to everyone without distinction. Although everyone was admitted to the so-called *initiations* <note>original: "Initien"</note>, or lesser mysteries; the so-called greater mysteries, which were called *teletai* <note>original: "τελετὰς"</note> or "perfections," were the sole province of the priesthood, and even within that group, a significant selection process took place. Furthermore, certain families in the state of Athens held such an exclusive right to specific duties and offices during the mysteries that these could be administered by no one else. During the times when Greece came under the rule of the Romans, Emperors, generals, and other high-ranking officials were also admitted to the great mysteries. However, since all these individuals either had held or still held priestly honors within the state, this did not infringe upon tradition or ancient ordinances, and the more sublime portion of the secrets remained still...

I do not wish to investigate to what extent this is right, or how it accords with the universal equality of mankind supported by nature. One is not more capable or virtuous simply because one stands in the service of religion, nor is one incapable or unvirtuous because one belongs to the class of the so-called laity <note>The author is critiquing the historical divide between the clergy and the common people.</note>. This may have come about because—as later happened among the Christians in the Middle Ages—the priesthood sought from the very beginning to maintain themselves in the possession of learning over the common people. Among the Egyptians, all scholars, physicians, and legal experts generally belonged to the priests. This also passed from them to the Jews. ...things which had to remain inaccessible to the <term>laity<note>German: "Layen"; referring here to those not initiated into the secret religious societies.</note></term>. Most of what we encounter among the ancients regarding the mysteries is found in the works of Greek writers. To be sure, they speak primarily of the **Eleusinian Mysteries** <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually in honor of Demeter and Persephone.</note>; but one can always take it as truth that the others were equal to them, and only diverged from them through certain modifications. Even if their origin did not prove this—since all were derived from one and the same source—one can nevertheless conclude it from other circumstances. **Herodotus** <note>An ancient Greek historian often called "The Father of History."</note> speaks in several passages of **Egyptian mysteries**, and in exactly the same manner as others speak of the Eleusinian ones. The description provided by **Apuleius** <note>A Roman writer and philosopher; his work "The Golden Ass" contains a famous account of initiation into the mysteries of Isis.</note>, as mystical as it may always be, coincides—as a connoisseur soon perceives—with the words dropped here and there by the Greeks.

The judgment of the Greeks concerning the secrets, both of the lesser and the greater mysteries, was of such a nature that one recognizes from it the great respect in which they were universally held by them. The Athenians believed that whoever had been initiated and had received instruction in the secrets would participate in divine honor after death. At least, this is how the commentator on *Aristophanes* explains it. The Greeks themselves likely did not count on receiving actual divine worship; but what was promised from the initiation was certainly nothing insignificant. ...attained true happiness. *) Indeed, it was even believed that one would become more pious, virtuous, and holy through these rites alone, thus ascribing to them a certain physical influence over the moral improvement of mankind. This was a prejudice that **Diogenes the Cynic** <note>A Greek philosopher known for his biting criticism of social conventions and hypocrisy.</note> already criticized; he could not comprehend why **Pataickion** the thief should be considered a virtuous man and enjoy a better fate in the afterlife simply because he had been an initiate, while **Themistocles** <note>The celebrated Athenian statesman and general.</note> should not. But this is not the first time, and certainly will not be the last, that the common people have attributed to external customs that which can only be expected from personal self-improvement—a task that requires far greater effort.

As is well known, everyone was initiated into the **Lesser Mysteries** <term>Lesser Mysteries: preliminary initiation rites held at Agrae, serving as a preparation for the Greater Mysteries of Eleusis</term>, and any man who had not sought initiation was not considered a good citizen. Warburton <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop and author of 'The Divine Legation of Moses', who argued that the ancient mysteries were designed to bolster civil law through the doctrine of a future state.</note> is correct when he asserts that the doctrines of future punishments and rewards were the reason for this. But this was certainly not the only reason. To be sure, the State cannot consider someone a good citizen who does not believe in a future life; the doctrines of punishments and rewards that await man in the hereafter—where the laws of the state can no longer reach—are, in a sense, the bond of civil society and the strongest support of the laws. However, these truths were not first made known within the "Lesser Mysteries," <note>The 'Lesser Mysteries' were the preliminary stage of initiation in ancient Greek religion, particularly at Eleusis.</note> nor were they entirely unknown to everyone else. The mythology of the poets and the common folk religion already contained them; they were merely made more poignant in the Mysteries through dramatic performances. Initiation into these Mysteries was, in a sense, to the pagan world what Confirmation <note>The author uses both 'Firmelung' (Catholic) and 'Confirmation' (Protestant) to explain the rite of passage to his readers.</note> is in the Christian world. Through this process, one became—if one may put it this way—a true member of the pagan church for the first time.

All of these are striking proofs of the extraordinary prestige in which the <term>Mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites, most notably those at Eleusis, which promised initiates better conditions in the afterlife.</note> were held among the ancients. ### III.

## Judgments of the Church Fathers on
## the Mysteries.

As favorably as the pagan writers consistently judge the mysteries; so disadvantageous are the descriptions of them found in the writings of the Church Fathers <note>The early influential theologians of the Christian Church, such as Augustine or Clement, who often wrote polemics against Greco-Roman religion</note>. In general, they view them as a farce of childish jugglery, with which composed and serious men should not occupy themselves. Clement of Alexandria <note>original: "Clemens von Alexandrien"; a prominent Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) known for his detailed, if critical, accounts of pagan rituals</note> says that when one finally uncovered the venerable objects that were carried about in the mysteries as matters of great importance and holiness, one beheld the most ridiculous and childish trifles. What does one find, says this Church Father, in their mysterious chests?

<note>Vocabulary: Judgments, Church Fathers, Mysteries, pagan writers, jugglery/deceptions, Clement of Alexandria</note>

<keywords>Church Fathers, Clement of Alexandria, pagan mysteries, religious polemics, ritual objects, early Christianity</keywords>

<meta>Continuity established with the previous page's discussion of ancient rites; catchword "sehen" (to see) connects to the following page's description of public exposure.</meta>

One cannot say that the Church Fathers <note>The influential Christian theologians and writers of the early centuries of the Church.</note>, who later judged the mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refer to secret religious rites of the ancient Greek and Roman world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which required initiation.</note> more favorably or leniently, were better informed about them than they had been previously, and thus changed their opinions. They could only have obtained these better insights through an actual initiation <term>initiation: the formal admission into a secret society or ritual group</term> into them, and they certainly did not undergo such a thing. One must entirely set this possibility aside. Whatever good they say of the mysteries was already known to them at the very time they were judging them unfavorably: at the very least, there is no probable cause that should have moved them to prefer later-received reports of their excellence over the evidence they believed they held to the contrary.

# On the Idolatry or Deification of those who have been Initiated.

The Christians, in turn, were for their part very dangerous people to the mysteries of the heathens <note>The term "heathens" or "pagans" refers here to the practitioners of ancient Greek and Roman polytheism.</note>. For what was whispered here into the ear—only after many trials and only to the initiated—concerning the highest and only God, the nature of the popular religion, and the future state, these Christians proclaimed loudly and publicly. This necessarily incensed the <term>Hierophants</term> <note>High priests who interpreted sacred mysteries, specifically at Eleusis.</note> and others of the priesthood; consequently, Christianity found in these men its most declared enemies and persecutors. Even if one further taught the actual philosophical religion within the "Great Mysteries," they nevertheless simultaneously supported idolatry with all their might, and it was precisely this which the Christians sought to overthrow. The former therefore became persecutors of the Christians, and the latter, in contrast, sought out everything they possibly could to present the mysteries—which they so greatly hated—in such a light that they would justly...

### IV.

## Relationship of the Mysteries to Philosophy.

It is well known that the philosophers of the ancient world, no less than the priesthood, a

<summary>This page explains how early Christian opposition to pagan mysteries eventually gave way to the adoption of similar secretive practices, and begins exploring the connection between ancient philosophy and mystery traditions.</summary>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>Continuity established with page 59; the catchword "man" (one/they) connects to the discussion of philosophical secrecy.</meta>

they maintained a certain *Secret Discipline* <note>original: "Disciplinam Arcani"; a practice of hiding certain knowledge from the uninitiated</note>, according to which some truths, or opinions, were presented to everyone without distinction, while others were only shared with those who had closer access to the philosophers and were, to some extent, scholars by profession, or intended to become such. One can say that among them a kind of double method of teaching existed, which made their entire presentation such a riddle that they spoke of one thing, but understood something entirely different by it.

There were various reasons for this. Firstly, it was believed that not all truths were useful to the common people, or could even be easily misused by them. Therefore, it often happened that the people were deceived through prejudices, and truths were most carefully concealed from them. Another cause was provided by the religion of those times itself. In Paganism <note>original: "Heidentum"</note>, people were no less strict against those who thought differently than they were later among the Christians. Had

<summary>This page discusses the "Secret Discipline" of ancient philosophers, explaining how they used a dual method of teaching to hide sensitive truths from the public to prevent misuse and avoid religious persecution.</summary>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>The catchword "gern" from the previous page likely completed a sentence regarding the willingness of ancient peoples to protect their traditions. This page continues the discussion on religious intolerance in antiquity.</meta>

Even if there was no formal Inquisition <note>The author refers to the ecclesiastical tribunal established in the Middle Ages to suppress heresy</note>, there were nonetheless courts of law before which crimes against religion were prosecuted. Such a body was the Areopagus in Athens. The history of the execution of Socrates is an example of pagan intolerance. What Plato dared to say in Sicily, he certainly would not have been permitted to say in Athens. Not infrequently, the presentation of philosophical truths bordered so closely on the doctrines of the Mysteries <note>Secret religious cults of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which required initiation</note> that the free teaching of these truths could certainly have been viewed as a betrayal committed against the latter.

This was also the reason why Socrates never wished to be initiated into the Mysteries. For, if he then put forward propositions that were consistent with the truths of the inner religion, he—as a man who was never initiated—could not be accused of having betrayed the secrets. The peculiar kind of esteem that was held in the oldest times toward all sciences <note>In this context, "sciences" (Wissenschaften) refers to organized branches of knowledge, including philosophy and theology</note>, which were viewed as a possession that for the common—

<summary>This page argues that religious intolerance existed in ancient Greece through legal bodies like the Areopagus, citing the trial of Socrates as a primary example. It explains Socrates' refusal to join secret Mysteries as a strategic move to maintain his freedom of speech without being accused of betraying sacred oaths.</summary>

<keywords>religious intolerance, Socrates, Areopagus, Athens, Plato, Mysteries, initiation, freedom of speech, ancient philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the origins of esoteric and exoteric teaching methods. This page continues by explaining the social and historical reasons for this distinction.</meta>

The fact that a large portion of the people did not belong to the inner circle at all contributed significantly to this dual method of teaching <note>The "dual method" refers to the distinction between "exoteric" (public) and "esoteric" (private) instruction</note>; especially since, in those times, the instruction of the philosophers—particularly among the Greeks—was entirely public, where everyone had permission to be present. It was therefore natural that certain truths were presented only in a hidden manner if they were believed to be unsuitable for the common man.

In the most ancient times, this dual method of teaching was practiced nowhere more frequently or taken further than among the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, known for their secretive communal life and mathematical mysticism</note>. In subsequent times, however, it was also in use among the so-called **Eclectic philosophers** of the **Alexandrian School**. Pythagoras had "inner" and "outer" students. To the latter, he gave only mere moral precepts; to the former, however, he revealed the higher truths of his philosophy. This also took place among the Alexandrians. They, too, speak very frequently in their writings of the *mysteries of philosophy*.

<summary>This page explains how the public nature of Greek philosophy led to the development of "hidden" teachings for elites, specifically highlighting the practices of the Pythagoreans and the Alexandrian School.</summary>
<keywords>dual teaching method, Pythagoras, Alexandrian School, esoteric knowledge, Greek philosophy, Eclecticism, pedagogical history</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion regarding the origins of ancient mysteries, following the catchword "weak" from the previous page.</meta>

As anyone will easily perceive, the following question arises here: in what relationship did the **mysteries of the ancients** stand to their **philosophy**, and was that which one learned in the former—after long trials and purifications—not basically just the same thing that the philosophers communicated to their listeners without these preparations?

**Warburton** <note>William Warburton (1698–1799), an English bishop and social critic known for his controversial theories on religion and law.</note> denies this outright: but certainly based on the incorrect principle according to which he believes that the mysteries were invented by **legislators** for the benefit of the **state**. In the mysteries, he says, one looked solely to the utility of civil society; among the philosophers, one looked solely to truths without concerning oneself with utility, and for that reason, the mysteries and the philosophy of the schools could not have been the same thing. (*) To me, this reason seems very

<summary>The author examines the historical relationship between ancient mystery cults and formal philosophy, specifically critiquing William Warburton's theory that mysteries were merely state-sponsored tools for social stability.</summary>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion regarding the relationship between truth and utility, following the catchword "guided" from the previous page.</meta>

weak, since one and the same thing can be used for very different ultimate purposes depending on the intention of those by whom it is handled. Furthermore, truth and utility are elements that cannot easily be separated from one another. Never yet has truth been given to the world without being beneficial to those who received it, and never yet has utility been lasting and thorough, except where it had its origin in the truth. It is also, at least as it seems to me, a completely unfounded accusation when this learned man <note>Likely referring to a specific contemporary critic or author mentioned in the preceding pages.</note> says that the philosophers and the founders of the Mysteries separated the two from each other—that the former despised usefulness, and the latter cared little for the truth. Of some philosophers of the Ionian school <note>The earliest school of Greek philosophy (6th century BCE), including figures like Thales and Anaximander, known for their focus on the natural substances of the universe.</note>, who lost themselves entirely in metaphysical and physical speculations, one can certainly say that they paid little attention to what consequences for the benefit of human society might be derived from the truths they recognized—

<summary>The author argues against the separation of truth and utility, refuting the claim that ancient philosophers and mystery cults ignored one in favor of the other.</summary>
<keywords>truth, utility, Ionian school, metaphysics, philosophy of science, social benefit, Mystery traditions</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>The catchword "einerley" (the same) from the previous page suggests a continuing discussion on the uniformity or diversity of philosophical systems.</meta>

...could be guided; but this certainly does not apply to all—not to the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras, known for their blend of mathematics and mysticism</note>, the Platonists, and especially not to all the philosophical parties that emerged from the Socratic school. It is true that the truth was hidden by those in possession of the Mysteries <note>Secret religious rites of ancient Greece, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries</note> and was not made known to the common man. However, besides the fact that the founders of the Mysteries were not the ones by whom the fabulous religion was invented and forced upon the people, one cannot say of them either that they looked only to utility and otherwise cared little for the truth. For not only were the most sublime truths presented in the innermost part of the Mysteries, but as much as the peace of the state allowed, they also sought to tear the common man away from his prejudices—as, for example, regarding the doctrine of the future state <note>The afterlife or the immortality of the soul</note>.

The whole question: whether the secrets [agree] with philosophy and its truths

...been one and the same? As anyone who possesses even a somewhat precise insight into both will soon realize, this can neither be straightforwardly affirmed nor denied. In this regard, one must take into account both the various types of mysteries <note>The "mysteries" were secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as those at Eleusis, which promised initiates special knowledge or a better afterlife.</note> of the ancient world, as well as the various philosophical sects. 

Some mysteries certainly could not boast the great advantage of containing matters of importance. This applies to all those that could be regarded, to a certain extent, as mere derivatives. Of this kind seem to have been the *Sacra Bonae Deae* <note>Latin: "Sacred rites of the Good Goddess." These were Roman rituals exclusively for women.</note> among the Romans, and among the Greeks those which were derived from the Eleusinian mysteries and from the Orgia <note>The "Orgia" refers here to the ecstatic rites of Dionysus, from which the modern word "orgies" originates, though in antiquity they were serious religious events.</note>. For although both the Eleusinian mysteries and the Orgia contained matters of importance, it seems that the same was not the case with those derived from them. Nor were they as famous or respected in the ancient world as those two were. Wherever the mysteries merely...

--- Page 94 ---

referred merely to the pagan folk religion <note>German: *Volksreligion*; the traditional public worship of the Greek and Roman gods</note>, or dealt with the history of the god to whom they were dedicated, then one cannot assume any agreement between philosophy and the mysteries: and it was only very late that the philosophers took the folk religion into consideration.

The same applies to philosophy and the various schools, or parties, that arose within it. Some of these had absolutely no connection with the secrets <note>German: *Geheimnissen*; referring here to the "Mysteries" or secret religious rites</note>. Socrates, for instance, did not wish to be initiated into the mysteries for the very reason that he might be hindered by the obligations required in them from presenting the truth; and from this one might very likely draw the conjecture that what he taught was, fundamentally, the same as what was presented in the mysteries; but this concerned only the two doctrines of the future state <note>The afterlife or the immortality of the soul</note> and the existence of a Supreme Being.

<summary>The author argues that while some philosophers eventually engaged with public religion, many schools—including Socrates—remained independent of the secret mysteries to preserve their intellectual freedom, despite sharing core beliefs about the soul and God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between ancient philosophical schools and the mystery cults, following the catchword "bey" (by/at) from the previous page.</meta>

Furthermore, this great man <note>Likely referring to Socrates, based on the context of moral philosophy</note> had banished everything from his philosophy that did not have a direct influence on the moral improvement of mankind; consequently, an uncommonly large number of things were omitted by him which otherwise constituted the subject matter of the mysteries. The same can also be said of the Aristotelian, Stoic, Epicurean, and several other schools. On the other hand, it is very probable that the two ancient sects of philosophy, namely the Ionian and the Italian, had an uncommonly large amount in common with the mysteries, and that they can rightly be regarded as actual derivatives of them. Thales of Miletus <note>c. 624 – 548 BC; often cited as the first philosopher in the Greek tradition</note> was instructed in Egypt by the priests, and brought their metaphysics <term>metaphysics: the branch of philosophy dealing with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being and knowing</term> and physics to the Greeks. (*) His principles regarding the gods, the origin, and the nature of things—as little as has been preserved for us—are fundamentally the very same that

It is enough to say that if one is somewhat informed about both—the philosophy of the ancients and their mysteries—one easily sees that they always offered each other a helping hand. It is not, therefore, <term>mysteriocrypsy</term> <note>From the Greek, meaning the concealment of mysteries or secret doctrines.</note> alone in which the philosophers agreed with the mysteries—although even this cannot be denied. ## V.

# Concerning several specific matters
# pertaining to the Mysteries.

Before I examine the secrets of the ancients themselves somewhat more closely, I must mention several specific matters that were characteristic of them and require a more detailed investigation. And to that end, the first point is that they were celebrated at night. That this applied to all of them collectively cannot, to be sure, be proven with complete certainty; however, it is very probable based on the agreement that prevailed among the various mysteries in so many other matters, and from their general origin. Regarding the Eleusinian <term>Mysteries (religious rites of ancient Greece involving secret initiations)</term>, it is certain that they were celebrated during the nighttime. When Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, Roman statesman and philosopher</note> explains the law,

bright day, they could have been easily discovered by anyone, and through which the entire ceremony would have lost much of its element of surprise; whereas, on the contrary, everything could be arranged in a more skillful and less noticeable manner at night.

This is therefore a true reason why the **mysteries** <note>original: "Geheimnisse"; refers to the secret religious rites of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries</note> were almost universally celebrated by the ancients during the nighttime. However, when I examine the whole matter more closely, I cannot possibly persuade myself to consider this the only reason. It is well known that the Christians in the second century carried the entire pagan **mystagogy** <note>The system of secret rituals and the instruction of initiates</note> over into the Church. 

The nocturnal worship service was a significant part of this. Although some have derived this practice from the fear of persecution, and the **Church Fathers** <note>Early influential Christian theologians</note> have also provided many a pious reason for it, its true origin is to be sought nowhere else than in the pagan mystagogy. "To the night," says **Synesius, Bishop of Ptolemais** <note>A Greek philosopher and bishop (c. 373 – c. 414 AD) who sought to reconcile Neoplatonism with Christianity</note>,

Unquestionably, the intention was to give these acts a more awe-inspiring appearance in such a manner, as the silence and darkness of night were seen as something that, in itself, would produce a certain holy shudder in the human heart.

These are, in my opinion, the two primary reasons for the nocturnal celebration of the <term>Mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites of ancient Greece, most notably those at Eleusis</note>. I do not mean to say by this that these were the only reasons. There may have been other grounds derived from the Mysteries themselves, and just as the <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>Early influential Christian theologians</note> did not lack pious interpretations of the Christian nocturnal worship services, one can easily imagine that the <term>Hierophants</term> <note>The high priests who interpreted sacred mysteries</note> of the Greeks were no less skilled in this regard. But how much do we truly know of this?

Another important circumstance is the deep and inviolable silence that was universally observed regarding everything that took place within the Mysteries. <term>Clement of Alexandria</term> <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who wrote extensively about pagan customs</note> says not only that they were never communicated except under a solemn pledge of silence, but one

---

<summary>The author discusses the reasons for the nocturnal nature of ancient Greek Mysteries and introduces the strict vow of silence required of all initiates.</summary>
<keywords>Mysteries, nocturnal worship, Church Fathers, Hierophants, Clement of Alexandria, secrecy, Euripides, religious silence</keywords>

One finds very striking evidence of this even among the pagan writers. The mere names *secret things* <note>original: "ἀπόῤῥητα" (aporrhēta) — things forbidden to be spoken</note> and *mysteries* <note>original: "μυστήρια" (mystēria) — things that must be kept silent</note> already indicate this. When Demonax <note>A Cynic philosopher known for his wit and independence</note> was accused before the Council at Athens, among other things, for not allowing himself to be initiated into the Mysteries, he excused himself by saying that he could not fulfill the conditions under which they were shared. Specifically, whether they were useful or harmful, he would not be able to keep them silent—either to encourage others to join if they were good, or conversely, to warn them against them. (*) Aeschylus <note>The famous Greek tragedian</note> was accused of betraying the secrets and saved himself only by proving that he had never been initiated. Diagoras <note>Known as "the Atheist," a poet and philosopher</note> had actually betrayed the Mysteries, and the Athenians promised a talent <note>A significant unit of currency in antiquity</note> to whoever would deliver him to them dead or alive. *Meiners* <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a philosopher and historian at Göttingen known for his studies on the history of religions.</note> considers this law of secrecy to be a matter for which an explanation cannot be given as easily, or in as satisfying a manner, as for the nocturnal celebration of the Mysteries. I am of the entirely opposite opinion, and this secrecy can be explained quite soon, as long as one is at least somewhat familiar with the subject of the Mysteries and their entire organization.

One important reason is certainly this: that in such a way, they wished to increase the esteem for the Mysteries and entice the people to accept them. This is at least what *Synesius* <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a philosopher-bishop whose writings often blended Neoplatonism with Christianity.</note>—who is not a witness to be dismissed in this matter—gives as a reason. The Church Fathers <note>Early influential Christian theologians and writers from the first few centuries AD.</note> are certainly not so lenient in their judgments. But they do not consider that this same argument could have been turned against them, as indeed actually happened. With a silence just as holy as that with which the pagans guarded their mysteries, the Christians also concealed the inner workings of their religion from all strangers ever since the so-called <term>Secret Discipline</term> <note>original: "Disciplina arcani"; a theological term for the practice of keeping certain rituals and doctrines hidden from non-believers.</note> was introduced among them. ...might be, is it not so much more beneficial that the truly holy and blessed vision be kept hidden? (*)

The holy and venerable prestige which they sought to give the mysteries through this means was certainly an important reason for this stubborn silence; but it was surely not the only one. If one casts an attentive glance at the so-called "Great Mysteries," one finds a far more important occasion for it. These were, in the most literal sense, <term>antitheses</term> <note>Direct opposites or contradictions</note> of the prevailing popular religion. As I shall show in the following, they not only exposed the generally prevailing error, but they also taught the truth. However, the popular religion was already so precisely and firmly connected with the entire State that one could not refute it, nor publicly establish the truth in its place, without simultaneously attacking the structure of the State itself in its various parts.

<summary>The author argues that the secrecy of ancient mysteries served a dual purpose: maintaining a sense of sacred awe and protecting the state from the subversive "truths" taught in private that contradicted the official public religion.</summary>
<keywords>Mysteries, Secrecy, Popular Religion, State and Religion, Clement of Alexandria, Subversion, Great Mysteries</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the origins of religious secrecy; this page continues by explaining the political and philosophical justifications for concealing the truth from the public.</meta>

It was therefore deemed necessary for the sake of general peace to allow error to remain, and to conceal the truth with the utmost care. Varro himself cited this reason: there are truths, he says, which are not beneficial for the state if they become generally known; and there are many things that are false, yet useful for the people to believe. For this reason, the Greeks entrusted their secrets to silence and to sacred walls. (*)

Another reason is likewise suggested by the internal nature of the mysteries. Even if their founders were not philosophers, as Clement of Alexandria <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who wrote extensively on the relationship between Greek philosophy and Christianity.</note> wishes to claim, it is nonetheless certain that the mysteries contained not only antitheses to the popular religion, but also included much scientific material from other—

<summary>This page discusses the deliberate concealment of religious truths by ancient states, citing Varro's belief that certain falsehoods are socially useful, and begins exploring the philosophical content of the Greek mysteries.</summary>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 90: The author is discussing the transmission of knowledge and the exclusivity of ancient learning. Catchword "nichts" (nothing) from the previous page is resolved in the opening sentence.</meta>

...had in other branches of scholarship <term>Gelehrsamkeit: refers to the formal learning and erudition of the educated classes</term>. In the ancient world, people thought differently than they do in the present. Sciences <note>The German "Wissenschaften" here refers to all systematic branches of knowledge, including philosophy and the arts</note> were not made known to the general public. It was believed that they would be degraded, and in a sense desecrated, if they were shared with the common people. The Greeks were, I believe, the first who made the sciences common property. I will not attempt to decide how extensive or how restricted the scholarship of the Egyptians may have been; but this much is certain: they kept their knowledge a secret. Those from whom they had received this knowledge likely proceeded in the same manner. It is to this reverence for the sciences—or, if one prefers, this envy—that we should likely attribute the deep silence observed regarding everything presented in the Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refers to secret religious rites of ancient Greece and Rome, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were closed to the uninitiated</note>. One might think that this should no longer have influenced the Greeks, who [derived] from their sciences...

# VI.
## Concerning the Lesser Eleusinian Mysteries.

Of all the other mysteries of the ancients, not as much has been passed down to posterity as those of <term>Eleusis</term> <note>A town near Athens, the site of the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece</note>. At most, we know a few symbols of the so-called <term>Orphic Mysteries</term> <note>Rites associated with the mythical poet Orpheus, focusing on the soul and the afterlife</note>. Regarding the Egyptian mysteries, individual pieces are known here and there, but they are not sufficient to assemble a complete whole from them. Likewise, only disconnected fragments of the so-called <term>Mithraic Mysteries</term> <note>A mystery religion centered on the god Mithras, practiced in the Roman Empire from the 1st to 4th centuries CE</note> have reached posterity.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>The author concludes the discussion on the necessity of absolute secrecy in ancient rites before beginning a detailed examination of the Eleusinian Mysteries, noting that they are better documented than Orphic, Egyptian, or Mithraic traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Eleusinian Mysteries, Orphic Mysteries, Egyptian Mysteries, Mithraic Mysteries, religious secrecy, ancient initiation</keywords>

<meta>The catchword "schreibt" (writes) from the previous page suggests a continuation of a discussion on historical documentation or authors. This page begins a specific focus on the Eleusinian Mysteries.</meta>

The most detailed accounts we possess concern the mysteries celebrated at <term>Eleusis</term> <note>An ancient city near Athens, famous as the site of the Eleusinian Mysteries dedicated to Demeter and Persephone.</note> in Greece. It does not follow from this that they were the most important; however, they were the most frequently visited. <term>Cicero</term> <note>The Roman statesman and philosopher (106–43 BCE) who was himself an initiate.</note> says that peoples from the most distant regions of the world came to be initiated into them. Consequently, they to some extent swallowed up all others that existed in Greece at the same time, or at the very least, obscured their fame. If one is familiar with these, one can form fairly accurate ideas of the other mysteries of the ancient world. <term>Meursius</term> <note>Johannes Meursius (1579–1639), a Dutch classical scholar.</note>, <term>Warburton</term> <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop who argued that the mysteries taught the unity of God.</note>, and <term>Meiners</term> <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian.</note> have provided the best accounts of them. Nevertheless, a connoisseur and precise judge will still find much to supplement here and there.

The secrets celebrated at Eleusis were divided into the Great and the Lesser. This has caused much trouble for all the writers who have written on this subject. For since the ancients did not always distinguish whether they were speaking of the former or the latter; so

# The Mysteries of Religion

...the secrets of religion, which every Christian must nevertheless know? The lesser mysteries <note>original: "kleinen Mysterien"</note> can therefore still bear this name, as they had to be kept secret from all who were not initiated; indeed, these uninitiated persons were not even permitted to enter the places to which the truly initiated had access.

Before the actual initiation into the lesser mysteries, certain customs took place which must be regarded as the actual preparations for them, and which had to be undertaken by all who wished to gain closer access to them. There is no doubt that through these preliminary exercises, they intended to elevate the mysteries themselves and give them a greater reputation among the common people. What these preliminary exercises consisted of cannot be said with perfect accuracy; yet it is certain that they were purifications or washings <note>The author refers to the Greek concept of 'katharsis' or ritual cleansing before entering sacred spaces</note>. Those to be initiated were led to the river Ilissus <note>A river in Athens, central to the rites of the Lesser Mysteries at Agrae</note>...

...the secrets of religion, which every Christian must nevertheless know? The lesser mysteries <note>The "lesser mysteries" (German: *kleinen Mysterien*) usually refer to the preliminary rites of the Eleusinian Mysteries, held at Agrae.</note> can therefore still bear this name, since they had to be kept secret from all who were not initiated; indeed, these uninitiated were not even permitted to enter the places to which the truly initiated had access.

Before the actual initiation into the lesser mysteries, certain customs preceded them, which had to be regarded as the actual preparations for the rites, and which had to be undertaken by all who wished to obtain closer access to them. There is no doubt that through these preliminary exercises, the mysteries themselves were intended to be placed on a higher level, and thereby given a greater prestige among the people. Exactly what these preliminary exercises consisted of cannot be said with perfect accuracy; yet this much is certain: they were purifications or washings <note>The term used is *Abwaschungen*, referring to ritual lustration or baptism-like cleansing.</note>. Those to be initiated were led to the river Ilissus <note>A river in Athens, central to the preliminary rites of the Eleusinian Mysteries.</note>,

# Completion and Sacrifice

*Teleiosis* <note>original: Τελείωσις</note>, or Completion. (\*) However, the naming convention used by Theon <note>Theon of Smyrna, a second-century philosopher who wrote on mathematical and philosophical initiation</note> is not incorrect in itself, as these preceding purifications figuratively represented a mystical death. One need only compare this with the two passages cited below from Tertullian and Augustine, where these purifications are discussed in general terms. (\*\*)

Then, a sacrifice was offered. During the initiation into the **Eleusinian Mysteries** <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually for the cult of Demeter and Persephone</note>, pregnant sows were sacrificed. This had a specific relationship to the deity under whose protection these mysteries were placed <note>Demeter, the goddess of agriculture and fertility</note>. In general, pregnant sows were sacrificed to this goddess. During the preliminary initiation into the Orgiastic rites, other sacrifices were undoubtedly used. Apuleius, in the account of his initiation—

# The Consecration and Sacrifices

The consecration likewise included sacrifices. Although he does not explicitly state what kind they were, it is understood from the entire arrangement of pagan worship that they were of the sort usually offered to Isis. <note>The author likely refers to Apuleius's descriptions of the Isiac mysteries.</note>

In addition to the sacrifices, those who wished to be initiated had to prepare themselves through prayer, fasting, and abstinence. Certain foods, such as fish, beans, and apples, were entirely forbidden; indeed, one was not even permitted to approach or touch these plants without becoming defiled. The prohibition of apples seems to stem from the story of Proserpina. <note>In Greek mythology, Proserpina (Persephone) was bound to the underworld after eating pomegranate seeds, often conflated with apples in later commentary.</note> The prohibition of fish and beans, however, is indisputably of Egyptian origin. From the answer that the initiates gave as a password upon their entry into the temple, one sees that they were given food and drink from the sacred vessels during their preliminary initiation. For the formula by which one recognized whether they had been initiated was this: "I have eaten from the..." <note>original: "ich habe aus dem..." — This likely refers to the *Synthema* or confession of the Eleusinian or Isiac mysteries.</note>

<summary>This page details the rigorous purification rituals for initiates of the mysteries of Isis, including specific dietary taboos against fish and beans and the use of secret passwords.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, initiation rites, ritual fasting, dietary laws, Proserpina, Egyptian influence, sacred vessels, mystery cults</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the initial stages of ancient mystery cults or the specific symbols used in Greek rituals. The catchword "nen" from the previous page connects to the beginning of this text.</meta>

->I have eaten from the **Tympanon** <note>A small hand-drum or tambourine used in ecstatic rituals</note>, drunk from the **Kymbalon** <note>Cymbals</note>, and carried the **Kernos** <note>A ceramic ritual vessel with multiple small cups attached to it</note>. Or: I have fasted, and drunk from the holy chalice: I have taken the chalice from the chest, and after I used it, placed it into the basket, and from the basket back into the chest.<-

All of these were elements of the **initiation** <term>Einweihung: the formal process of entering a secret religious society</term>; however, the order in which they followed one another, like several other parts that occurred therein, remains entirely unknown. In the **Samothracian mysteries** <note>Secret religious rites held on the island of Samothrace, famous throughout the Greek world</note>, a formal confession of sins preceded the rites. Plutarch therefore relates of **Lysander** the **Lacedaemonian** <note>A Spartan; Lacedaemon was the formal name for the Spartan state</note> that when the priest commanded him to confess his sins before his initiation, he asked: "Who demands this? You or the gods?" When the priest replied that the gods commanded it, Lysander said: "Well then, you go away; if the gods ask me, I shall tell it to them—"

Meiners <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian known for his work on the history of religions.</note> says it is uncertain whether the initiates were scourged <note>whipped or lashed as a ritual act</note>, which was customary in the mysteries celebrated by the Pheneatians <note>inhabitants of Pheneus in Arcadia</note> in honor of Ceres <note>The Roman name for Demeter, goddess of agriculture and the harvest.</note>, and which corresponded exactly with the Eleusinian Mysteries. <meta>The previous page discussed the general nature of the ancient mysteries; this page continues by detailing the specific rituals and requirements for initiation into the inner sanctum.</meta>

An immense amount of obscurity is spread over this part of the mysteries. Almost nothing is known of the customs observed therein, except that those to be initiated wore myrtle wreaths upon their heads and were required to wash their hands immediately upon entering the temple. Furthermore, a herald proclaimed that they should approach only with a pure soul, clean hands, and speaking the Greek dialect. <note>The requirement of the "Greek dialect" likely served to exclude "barbarians" or foreigners from the most sacred Athenian rites.</note> (*) Two elements, however, seem to have preceded this: namely, a solemn vow of silence toward all who were not initiated, and a promise to lead a virtuous life. Both of these are too closely connected with the rite to have been separated from it. But over everything else, time has drawn the curtain. Perhaps everything perceived within the temple itself constituted the initiation <term>epopteia: the highest stage of initiation where the mystery is revealed</term> and was accompanied by trials and purifications. It also appears that certain signs were given to those who had been initiated in such a manner

That the great truths of a **future state** <note>The afterlife or the immortality of the soul</note> were taught in the Mysteries suffers not the slightest doubt. Warburton further cites the formula with which the initiates were dismissed once the initiation was completed. These were the words: *Konx Ompax* <note>original: "Κογξ ὀμπαξ". And he notes in this regard that these two barbaric words prove that the mysteries did not have a Greek origin. Le Clerc believed they were Phoenician, and should be pronounced *Kots Omphats*, which is supposed to mean: "watch and abstain from evil" (*).

But why these Phoenician words in Greek mysteries, since it is certain that the Greeks did not receive their secrets from the Phoenicians, but rather from the Egyptians? Moreover, where such a distortion of words is already assumed, it will not be difficult to give them a meaning from any language one chooses. The dramatic performances were, to a certain extent, already lessons in themselves—a form of instruction that was suited to the sensibilities of the common man and was all the more impactful for it. It is also true that almost everything was expressed through outward forms and ceremonies, to such an extent that without initiation, even the most virtuous of men was promised no happy fate in the future <note>Referring to the afterlife or the state of the soul after death.</note>.
