### VII.
## Concerning the Great Mysteries.

Not all at once, says Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher.</note>, are certain sacred rites shared. Eleusis <note>The site of the Eleusinian Mysteries, the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note> holds something back, which is only made known to those who approach the mysteries once again. Nature, too, does not make her sacred shrines known all at once. We already consider ourselves to be initiates, yet we are still standing in the vestibule. Those mysteries are not revealed to everyone without distinction. They are held back and are locked away in the innermost sanctuary. (*) Clement of Alexandria <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150 – 215 AD) who sought to unite Greek philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> is therefore perfectly right,

<margin>(*) Seneca, *Natural Questions*, Book VII, Chapter 31. <note>original: "Seneca Quaestion. Natural. VII. 31."</note></margin>

<summary>This page discusses the gradual revelation of truth, comparing the secrets of nature to the ancient Greek mysteries of Eleusis as described by Seneca. It emphasizes that true knowledge is a sacred process that requires repeated effort and is not immediately accessible to everyone.</summary>
<keywords>Seneca, Clement of Alexandria, Eleusinian Mysteries, philosophy of nature, initiation, sacred knowledge, hidden truths</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the distinction between the lesser and greater mysteries; this page continues that thought, focusing on the transformative nature of the "Greater Mysteries."</meta>

...if he regards the lesser mysteries as preparations and preliminary exercises for the greater ones.

These greater mysteries were, in the truest sense, the inner core of the pagan religion; and what was told to the initiates here was of such a nature that, had it become generally known, a significant revolution would have had to occur in the external religion and in the state itself. They were the epitome of those insights which, for various reasons, were withheld from all the uninitiated, and which were made known to only a very few—namely, only to the most select part of the priesthood and the rulers. To them, therefore, in the truest sense, belong the names **the full vision** <note>original: "ἐποψία" (epopsia); the highest stage of initiation in the Eleusinian Mysteries</note>, **things of which one must not speak** <note>original: "ἀπόῤῥητα" (apporhēta); secret or forbidden doctrines</note>, **completion** <note>original: "τελετή" (teletē); a rite of passage or consecration</note>, and so forth. This was the actual scientific <note>The author uses "scientific" (wissenschaftlich) here in the older sense of systematic, organized knowledge or scholarship</note> part; everything else was merely images, as it were, hieroglyphs, and sensory representations: but here, the curtain that had previously hidden the truth was—

<summary>This page describes the "Greater Mysteries" of ancient paganism as a secret, revolutionary body of knowledge reserved for the elite priesthood and rulers, distinct from the symbolic rituals shown to the public.</summary>
<keywords>Greater Mysteries, initiation, priesthood, secret doctrines, epopsia, ancient religion, state revolution</keywords>

And Gregory of Nazianzus <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and influential theologian</note> says that no one obtained access to them who had not previously endured certain trials and mortifications, of which he names twelve distinct stages (*). Perhaps they compensated for the careful concealment of the method of initiation once it was no longer possible to withdraw all the inner truths of the "Great Mysteries" from the eyes of strangers in such a way that nothing at all could be known of them.

The <term>teletai</term> <note>original: "τελετάς" - the rites of initiation or completion</note> were always regarded as the epitome of all, and the most sublime, branches of knowledge. Clement of Alexandria, who otherwise judges the mysteries so contemptuously, nevertheless says of them:

> The great secrets concern all things in general. Nothing more is left to be learned; instead, one now learns to understand nature and its works (*).

And in that same regard, the author of the *Etymologicum Magnum* <note>A 12th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of Greek words and their origins</note> says that in these rites, one received correct concepts of the gods (**). If one is to form a concept of what was presented in these great mysteries based on these two important statements, the knowledge to which the initiate was led was partly theological and partly physical in content. One cannot wonder at this connection for long: for in the philosophical schools of the ancients,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the nature of ancient mysteries, specifically focusing on the scope of knowledge imparted during the final stages of initiation.</meta>

The <term>initiations<note>original: "τελετάς" (teletas) – the final rites or completions of the mystery religions</note></term> were always regarded as the epitome of all, and the most sublime, branches of knowledge. Clement of Alexandria, who otherwise judges the mysteries so contemptuously, nevertheless says of these: "The great mysteries concern all things in general. Nothing more is left to be learned; instead, one now learns to understand nature and its works" (*).

And in that same regard, the author of the *Etymologicon Magnum* <note>A 12th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of word origins</note> says that in these rites, one received correct concepts of the gods (**). If one is to form a concept of what was presented in these great mysteries based on these two important statements, then the knowledge to which the <term>initiate<note>original: "Eingeweihten" – those who have been "let in" to the secret rites</note></term> was led was partly theological and partly physical in content. One cannot wonder at this connection for long: for in the philosophical schools of the ancients, it was...

Both were most precisely connected with one another. I cannot let an observation pass by on this occasion. It is the following: 

What we know of the **Great Mysteries** <note>The "Great Mysteries" likely refers to the Eleusinian Mysteries or similar ancient Greek initiations which promised secret knowledge of the divine.</note> of the ancients concerns almost exclusively the religious insights revealed therein; on the other hand, we know almost nothing of the rest. Why is this? Were those other parts perhaps of less importance? I do not believe so; rather, those were the very things that most required the **seal of secrecy**. I find the cause for this nowhere else but in **Christianity**. 

When this arose, these formerly kept-secret doctrines were partly uncovered by the Christians, and partly it happened here and there by the **Pagans** <note>original: "Heiden"</note> themselves. In this way, they endeavored to save their religion from the bitter reproaches that the Christians made against them with so much reason, and to prove that their religion was not as absurd and irrational as it appeared to be on the surface.

<summary>The author argues that the secrets of ancient mystery religions were only revealed because of the rise of Christianity, as both Christians and Pagans sought to expose or defend the underlying rationality of ancient beliefs.</summary>
<keywords>Great Mysteries, Christianity, Pagans, secrecy, religious history, apologetics</keywords>

# The First Stage of the Mysteries

The first thing that occurred in the great mysteries was the discovery of the errors within the popular religion. It was demonstrated that the external religion, to which the common people adhered, was not the true one. The <term>Mystagogue</term><note>A priest who initiates others into the mysteries</note>, or <term>Hierophant</term><note>The high priest who reveals sacred objects</note>, showed that this entire religion of images was merely the cover, as it were, or the curtain, behind which the true religion was hidden. *Jupiter*, *Juno*, *Minerva*, *Mars*, *Venus*, and *Mercury*, and the entire high retinue of gods who were worshipped by the ignorant and superstitious rabble, were not actually gods, but had originally been mortal men. It was shown where they were born, where they had lived, and even their tombs were still to be found there. However, certain services they had rendered to the human race had prompted the people to honor their memory, and from this, they eventually attained actual divine worship. *Cicero* states this explicitly and gives a very clear indication that this was taught in the great mysteries. Is it not, he says,

If I dared to examine the ancient accounts, and among those primarily the ones recorded by the Greek writers, one would find that even the gods of the highest rank ascended to heaven from among us. Just inquire as to what kind of graves are shown in Greece! Remember, since you are an initiate <note>A person who has been admitted into the secret rites of a mystery religion.</note>, what is said about this in the Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refers to secret religious cults of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which promised knowledge of the afterlife.</note>, and you will see how far this extends. — It is true that there is no nation that does not believe in gods; but it is also certain that very many have entirely erroneous concepts of the gods. (*) Did the Mystagogues <note>A priest who initiates others into the mysteries.</note>, who were after all priests, then present themselves as frauds by whom the people were kept in error? — I believe not: for that would have brought little honor upon themselves.

They presented themselves as what they truly were in a certain sense: namely, as those who had preserved the true religion. The popular religion, on the other hand, was presented for what it was: namely, as something that owed its origin partly to the poets, partly to the legislators, and partly also to the philosophers. It had been established according to the state and the entire constitution of the people, and now could not be abolished without a great revolution. This is how Scaevola <note>Quintus Mucius Scaevola, a Roman jurist and Pontifex Maximus</note> explains it in the works of Varro, whom Augustine cites in his books *On the City of God* <note>original: "De Civitate Dei"</note> (*). Varro therefore also believes that there are many religious truths that must be concealed from the people, and that, conversely, there are errors that the people must be allowed to keep.

The entire veil of error and superstition was thus first stripped away from the eyes of the initiated in the Great Mysteries <note>The "Great Mysteries" refers to the Eleusinian Mysteries or similar secret ancient Greek religious rites</note>—

It is always to be regretted that these insights and explanations have been lost along with the mysteries in which they were hidden, and have been withdrawn from the eyes of posterity. # On the Nature of the Mysteries

When it is said that in these rites one makes known the nature of things rather than the character of the gods, and likewise when *Clement of Alexandria* <note>Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150 – c. 215 AD), a Christian theologian who wrote extensively on Greek mysteries in his work 'Protrepticus'.</note> says that in the mysteries one learns to know all of nature and its works, these statements do not yet seem to me to prove this specific method <note>The author is referring to a hypothesized "scientific" or "physical" method of instruction.</note>. In both passages, the discussion concerns not so much the style of teaching, but rather the fact that the doctrine of the gods of the popular religion <term>Volksreligion: the public, traditional polytheistic worship of the state</term> is explained through nature, representing the gods as personified objects of the natural world. 

A passage cited by *Meiners* <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian of religion.</note> from *Galen* <note>Aelius Galenus (129 – c. 216 AD), the prominent Greek physician and philosopher.</note> might seem to argue more strongly in favor of this. However, if one examines this passage with precision, it says nothing other than that in the Eleusinian and Samothracian mysteries <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries were rites for Demeter and Persephone; the Samothracian Mysteries involved the "Great Gods" or Cabeiri.</note>, one investigates the nature of things and from this makes visible the wisdom, power, and goodness of the creator of animals. (*) From this, however, that aforementioned method is not yet proven; and what one 

-> learned <-

about the nature of things in the mysteries,

If one considers them merely as <term>stage-players</term> <note>original: "Gaukler" or "Taschenspieler," referring to deceptive performers or charlatans</note>, what then should one think of the **Jews**? Among them, too, the **priesthood** was bound to a sacred tribe, as it was in **Egypt**, and the high-priestly dignity passed from father to son. Even among the **Greeks**, various offices within the **Mysteries** were likewise hereditary, as I mentioned above, and yet **Meiners** <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a philosopher and historian at Göttingen known for his skeptical views on ancient wisdom</note> makes an exception in the case of the Greeks. Among them, too, only a few from the priesthood were admitted to the **Great Mysteries**, and yet he does not consider their mysteries to be those that consisted of empty ceremonies and religious sleight-of-hand, but rather to contain scientific knowledge.

In order to show that the **Egyptians** possessed no scientific knowledge in their mysteries, Meiners cites the following reasons:

1) If they had so much to hide, why can one not name at least some of these insights and specify them precisely? I answer to this: what we know of the

Greek mysteries are also merely scattered words. But how can the conclusion be drawn: we cannot state with certainty what knowledge the Egyptians taught in their mysteries, therefore they possessed none at all? Can it surprise us that we can say so little for certain about them, since not a single authentic, ancient, Egyptian monument that is intelligible has come down to us? — **Brucker** <note>Johann Jakob Brucker (1696–1770), a highly influential historian of philosophy known for his "Historia Critica Philosophiae."</note>, it is further stated, specifies a secret theology: but **Brucker** did not know that the Egyptians had no teachers, but rather holy masters of ceremonies. — I think this must first be proven. The proof, however, might well be invalid: that the Egyptians had no scientific knowledge (which certainly presupposes teachers) because they had no teachers, but only holy masters of ceremonies.

Even if it were always contrary to the laws of moral nature—even if jugglers and fools taught in caves and crypts while *Socratics* <note>Followers or students of Socrates; the original uses the plural "Socratesse".</note> taught in public places—the very same thing occurred among the *Greeks*. And at the very time when the *Socratics* were teaching publicly in *Greece*, the *Greeks* concealed their secret theology, natural philosophy, and other sciences, sharing them only with those who were initiated into the Great Mysteries. Furthermore, the *philosopher* who was initiated was forced to remain silent about the very truths that he could have presented without hesitation as an uninitiated person. The *Egyptians* certainly had the same reasons for acting as the *Greeks* did, and the folk religion was no less an insurmountable obstacle for the former than it was for the latter.

The Egyptians spoke to Herodotus <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BCE), often called the "Father of History," who wrote extensively about Egypt.</note> without hesitation regarding the movement of celestial bodies and the arrangement of the year. Even in Greece, the sciences were free and taught publicly, and yet scientific secrets existed alongside them. Even Herodotus himself encounters things in his *History* that he feels a hesitation to speak of.

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the validity of Egyptian secret knowledge; this page continues the rebuttal against the historian Christoph Meiners.</meta>

...see that the previously cited reasons are not sufficient to deny the Egyptians scientific knowledge within their mysteries, nor to reduce them merely to the status of sleight-of-hand magicians.

Meiners himself, however, has elsewhere conceded to the Egyptians what he denies here, and has declared that they possessed certain knowledge distinct from the fables of the popular religion <note>The "popular religion" refers to the public myths and polytheism of the common people, as opposed to the "mysteries" of the priesthood.</note>, which was not permitted to be generally known. He admits that they preserved this knowledge in books that were not only unintelligible to the masses, but were also withheld from them; and that they communicated this knowledge in certain mysteries only to a few, and even then only after lengthy trials. This knowledge included the ancient history of the empire, observations from the natural history of their land, their teachings on the movements of the celestial bodies, and their opinions on the origin of the world as well as the nature of the Deity. --- Page 184 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 150: The author continues the argument regarding the transmission of ancient wisdom and the historical validity of the origins of mystery cults.</meta>

If one wishes to trample historical faith underfoot, one cannot think otherwise. And if it is true—as the Greeks themselves say regarding the origin of their mysteries, in which the Egyptians also concurred—that the secrets of the Greeks were actually of Egyptian origin; (*) then it is impossible to grant scientific knowledge to the Greek mysteries while simultaneously denying it entirely to those from whom the Greeks had merely received them. 

There is, however, no reason at all why the Greeks—who were otherwise so jealous of everything that could bring them honor—should have attributed the invention of the mysteries specifically to the Egyptians if they had originated at home with them. It is true, they also recount that Ceres <note>The Roman name for Demeter, the goddess of agriculture and the harvest, central to the Eleusinian Mysteries.</note> brought the mysteries to them: but who does not see at the same time that this belongs to the realm of legends?

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the transmission of theological knowledge from Egypt to Greece, specifically addressing the purity of their monotheistic concepts.</meta>

<sig>VIII.</sig>

If one grants to the Greeks—who had received the mysteries from the Egyptians—that they presented correct concepts of God within those same mysteries, and revealed the single true God to the initiated; then the question regarding the Egyptians is as good as decided. Everything that *Meiners* <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a philosopher and historian at Göttingen who often argued against the idea that ancient mysteries held high moral or monotheistic truths.</note> has said against this in the 13th chapter of his *History of Religion* is self-refuting; it is even refuted by his own explanation that the Egyptian priests taught the nature of the Deity in their mysteries, even if they did not possess such a pure natural theology <note>Natural theology refers to knowledge of God obtained through reason and the observation of nature, rather than through supernatural revelation.</note> as that which *Pythagoras* and several others are said to have brought out of Egypt.

This entire matter deserves a closer investigation, in which the question of what the original religion of this people was would necessarily come into consideration. However, such an investigation would lead me too far away from my purpose. I shall therefore proceed to other subjects of the Great Mysteries.

## Further Investigation into the Great Mysteries.

Closely connected with the doctrine of the existence of a supreme God—the creator and ruler of the entire world—was the doctrine of demons, or intermediary natures. I might almost say that the teaching of the existence of such beings, who stand as it were in the middle between the Divinity and humanity, is so natural that one cannot hear the former doctrine without simultaneously stumbling upon the latter. Whichever way one turns in the religious history of ancient peoples, one finds everywhere the accepted principle that there exist certain invisible beings who are subject to a higher power and who exert their influence particularly upon this world.

That the nature of demons <note>In this historical context, "demons" refers to the Greek *daimones*—spiritual beings or tutelary spirits that bridge the gap between gods and mortals—rather than the modern concept of evil spirits.</note> was taught in the Mysteries is likewise stated by the an-

<summary>This page explores the theological connection between the belief in a supreme creator and the existence of intermediary spirits or "demons" within ancient mystery traditions. The author argues that the concept of beings mediating between the divine and human realms is a universal feature of ancient religious history.</summary>
<keywords>Great Mysteries, demons, intermediary beings, theology, ancient religion, creator, divinity</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the origins of religious doctrines; this page continues the exploration of the hidden nature of the divine as taught in ancient mysteries.</meta>

ten. But even if they did not say it, one could very soon surmise it with the greatest probability. The same thought, which the Alexandrian philosophers <note>A school of thought in Hellenistic Egypt, such as Neoplatonism, which blended Greek philosophy with Eastern mysticism</note> later adopted—that the highest deity is a hidden being who, as it were, keeps himself perpetually withdrawn into his own unity—was certainly a fundamental principle of the Mysteries. Even in the most ancient times, this opinion held sway in Egypt. The government of this world had been handed over to certain invisible powers. It was therefore natural that one sought to give the initiates concepts of these beings who were subordinate to the highest God; and from this arose the entire doctrine of the secrets regarding the nature of demons <note>original: "Dämonen"; here referring to "daemons" or intermediary spirits in the classical sense, rather than evil entities</note>, their classes, occupations, and various relationships toward God and toward men, between whom they formed, as it were, the middle ground.

Not everything that was told of the gods in mythology <note>original: "Fabellehre"</note> could be derived from the hero-

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the internal structure of ancient religious systems; this page continues the analysis of how the Mysteries supported Paganism against the rise of Christianity.</meta>

...they were, in essence, the very strongest defenders of the same. It is remarkable that Paganism <note>original: "Heidentum"; referring here to the polytheistic religions of the Greco-Roman world</note> only completely collapsed once the Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refers to secret religious rites, such as those at Eleusis, which required initiation</note> were entirely abolished. As long as these still persisted, Paganism always possessed an internal and very powerful support. Consequently, the Christians exerted every imaginable effort not only to shake and topple their prestige by refuting the Mysteries, but also, from the time of *Constantine the Great* <note>Roman Emperor (r. 306–337 AD) who first legalized Christianity</note> onward, to suppress them entirely by force. In the second century, one can almost say that the servants of the Mysteries formed an *alliance* with the Platonic philosophers against *Christianity*.

I have said that, through this doctrine, the popular religion <note>original: "Volksreligion"; the public, state-sanctioned worship of the many gods</note> found its defenders in the very people who seemed to dispute it upon the discovery of the doctrine of the existence of a single God. For here, some gods of the popular religion were depicted as demons <note>original: "Dämonen"; in the Platonic sense, these were intermediary spirits between gods and men, not necessarily evil</note>...

presented as mediators between the highest God and humanity, as <term>sub-regents</term><note>German: Unterregenten; refers to subordinate deities or spiritual governors</note> to whom the God, who hides himself in his own unity, had handed over the government of this world. They were seen as beings who acted upon the whole of nature; one had to avert their harmful influences through all kinds of means of reconciliation, while bringing about their fortunate influences through sacrifices, prayers, and all manner of ceremonies. In general, it was believed in paganism that the statues of the gods, if they had been properly consecrated, would be inhabited by a certain spirit or <term>genius</term>. wandering <note>original: "Seelenwanderung" – metempsychosis or the transmigration of souls</note>, is uncertain. --- Page 195 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the initial stages of the mysteries; this page transitions into the deeper scientific and philosophical knowledge held by the priesthood.</meta>

If one considers the Great Mysteries in light of what has been said thus far, everything presented within them still had a direct relationship to religion. However, one must remember that in those times, all wisdom and all scholarly knowledge were in the hands of the priesthood. The clergy maintained their prestige precisely by asserting themselves as the exclusive possessors of all scientific knowledge. Thus, it is easy to see that the matter did not rest with religion alone, but that other insights also formed the subject of the "Greater Secrets."

<margin>(*) STROMATA, Book V.</margin>
For this reason, Clement of Alexandria <note>Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150 – 215 AD), a Christian theologian who sought to unite Greek philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> says that the Great Mysteries concerned all things in general, and that within them, one came to know Nature and her works (*). These branches of knowledge were, on one hand, connected to religious explanations; just as mythology <note>original: "Fabellehre"</note>, when subjected to a correct and precise interpretation, points toward ancient history and toward—

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the origins of religious systems; this page continues by linking the study of spirits to the study of the natural world.</meta>

Just as the doctrine of spirits <note>original: "Dämonen"; in this context, referring to the Greek 'daimon' or intermediary spirits rather than modern 'demons'</note> guided them, it also naturally led to a knowledge of nature, since several deities of the folk religion were nothing other than personified objects of nature. Dionysius of Halicarnassus says, therefore, that the fables of the Greeks indicated the works of nature under an allegory. (*) Plutarch says the very same thing, and to that end appeals to the fables of the Egyptians and to the poems of Orpheus. This was the general nature of the cosmogonies <note>theories regarding the origin of the universe</note> of the ancients. Heraclitus therefore deposited his book on nature in the Temple of Diana at Ephesus. Tatian calls them poems, and from what Clement of Alexandria says of them, one sees that they were of mysterious <note>or "mystical/initiatory"</note> content. Had they not—like all ancient cosmogonies—concerned the gods of the folk religion, there would have been no reason at all for their secrecy, since otherwise the sciences were taught publicly among the Greeks.

(*) <note>original: "Dion. Halic. Antiq. Rom. Lib. I. p. 92."</note> Dionysius of Halicarnassus, *Roman Antiquities*, Book I, page 92.

If one considers the Great Mysteries based on what has been said thus far, everything presented within them still had a direct relationship to religion. However, one must remember that in those times, all wisdom and all scholarly knowledge were in the hands of the priesthood. The priesthood maintained its prestige precisely by asserting itself in the exclusive possession of all scientific knowledge. Thus, it is easy to see that the matter did not simply rest there, but that other insights also formed the subject of the "Greater Secrets."

Just as the doctrine of <term>demons<note>In this historical context, "demons" refers to the Greek 'daimones'—spiritual beings or intermediaries between gods and men, not necessarily evil spirits.</note></term> guided them, it also naturally led to a knowledge of nature, since several deities of the folk religion were nothing other than personified objects of nature. Dionysius of Halicarnassus says, therefore, that the fables of the Greeks indicated the works of nature under an allegory. (*) Plutarch says the very same thing, and to that end refers to the fables of the Egyptians and the poems of Orpheus. This was the general nature of the <term>cosmogonies<note>Stories or theories regarding the origin of the universe.</note></term> of the ancients. Heraclitus therefore deposited his book on nature in the Temple of Diana at Ephesus. Tatian calls them poems, and from what Clement of Alexandria says of them, one sees that they were of mysterious <insert>or secret</insert> content. Had they not—like all ancient cosmogonies—involved the gods of the folk religion, there would have been no reason at all for their concealment, since otherwise the sciences were taught publicly among the Greeks.

But it was not only the works of nature, insofar as they had a relationship to religion and stood in connection with it, that were an object of the Great Mysteries; rather, it was natural history, astronomy, and whatever else might belong to those fields in general. Regarding the Greeks and their Mysteries, this is perhaps not so much to be believed, for among them the sciences were public and universal. However, regarding the Egyptians, it is certain. For even if they instructed the likes of Plato, Herodotus, and other traveling Greeks in some matters without requiring initiation from them beforehand, they nevertheless made secrets out of the majority of their sciences. In the times of Clement of Alexandria <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who wrote extensively on Greek philosophy and Egyptian customs.</note>, there were 42 such books in existence which were attributed to Thoth, or Hermes <note>Thoth is the Egyptian god of wisdom; the Greeks identified him with their god Hermes, hence the "Hermetic" books.</note>, and from the account he gives of their content, one can see what they actually occupied themselves with. Regardless of what one may think concerning the authenticity of these books, one cannot deny—based on other testimonies from **Strabo**, **Plato**, **Diogenes Laertius**, **Isocrates**, **Plutarch**, and other writers—that astronomy, astrology, geometry, natural history, and medicine belonged to the branches of knowledge which the Egyptian priests kept locked away within their mysteries. These subjects were communicated in their entirety only to those who had gained admission to those secrets. And although it was not the case among the Greeks that scientific knowledge was bound only to a sacred tribe and remained the sole property of the priesthood, as it was in Egypt; one can nevertheless conclude with great probability from the translation <note>In this context, "translation" refers to the historical transfer or migration of cultural practices from one civilization to another.</note> of the Egyptian mysteries to Greece, that among the Greeks as well, the Great Mysteries did not merely extend to matters concerning religion alone, but rather encompassed much more. One can always follow the trail here to see how some parts of these mysteries were discovered while others remained hidden. The Greek scholars who traveled to Egypt were not so much concerned with religious knowledge as they were with philosophical sciences. Through this means, this specific part of their mysteries has become better known to posterity than what they taught concerning the gods. The Church Fathers <note>Early Christian theologians (1st–8th centuries) who often wrote polemics against Greco-Roman religion.</note>, who fought against paganism, cared little for the philosophical knowledge contained in the mysteries, but instead attacked the theology alone. Because of this, and through the defenses written by the pagans, many things concerning the religious truths of the Greek mysteries have become known, while everything else, by contrast, has remained concealed.

Meiners <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a philosopher and historian at Göttingen known for his studies on the history of religions.</note> also raises the question of whether principles of good conduct and precepts of virtue were presented in the Great Mysteries? He declares this to be uncertain. (*)

## IX.
# Concerning the Mithraic Mysteries of the Persians.

When I examine the mysteries of the ancients with some precision, it is impossible for me to entirely bypass the so-called Mithraic mysteries <note>The cult of Mithras, centered on a Zoroastrian angelic divinity, which later became a major mystery religion in the Roman Empire.</note> of the Persians. Although I do not believe that they stand in any connection with the Greek mysteries, they nevertheless deserve some attention. It is remarkable that various accounts of them appear among the ancient writers, but our modern discoveries say not the slightest thing about them.

Meiners <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a philosopher and historian at Göttingen known for his works on the history of religions.</note> believes that the oldest Persians, up until the time of Herodotus, had no mysteries at all. Herodotus, Xenophon, and Strabo <note>Three foundational Greek historians and geographers whose works provide the primary Western accounts of ancient Persian customs.</note>

<summary>The author introduces the Mithraic mysteries of Persia, noting their distinctness from Greek traditions and citing the skepticism of historian Christoph Meiners regarding their antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Mithraic mysteries, Persia, Christoph Meiners, Herodotus, Xenophon, Strabo, ancient religion</keywords>

Persians, and this is also, as Meiners <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a philosopher and historian at Göttingen known for his "History of Religions."</note> believes,
entirely at odds with their state and religious con-
stitution. Herodotus describes them as a sav-
age and nomadic people. They had neither
temples nor altars; they worshipped the sun, moon,
fire, water, and earth, and with such
a constitution, among such a people, who
worshipped no gods in human form,
mysteries would naturally have to be omitted. (*)

As much as I would like to give Herodotus my

much that is contradictory and untrue.
For at one moment it is said that the Persians are quite rough

their meals, but rather eat whatever
comes their way, and drink no wine at all, but
only water. (**) Then again it is said,
on the other hand, that they drink a great deal of wine, and in
a drunken spirit [decide] things of the greatest

# On the Importance of the Persians

They are, to a certain extent, a nomadic people, yet they already possess such civil institutions <note>The author uses "bürgerliche Einrichtungen" to describe a transition from tribal life to organized urban law.</note> that lepers must keep themselves away from their cities and, if they are foreigners, are kept away from the borders. In later times, they are said to have first learned the worship of Venus Urania—whom they call Mithra—from the Assyrians and Arabs. And yet, the name Mithradates <note>A name meaning "given by Mithra."</note>, which was the name of the shepherd by whom the young Cyrus was raised, already indicates that a deity named Mithra was worshipped among the Persians much earlier, and that they did not first receive this alleged Venus Urania from the Assyrians and Arabs. In general, however, one cannot read Herodotus's accounts of the Persians without encountering a great deal of incorrect and absurd information everywhere. This includes the claim that their Magi <term>Magi: the priestly caste of ancient Persia and Zoroastrianism</term> consider it a merit if they kill many gnats, snakes, ants, and crawling animals.

That the Persians had no human-like deities, such as the Greeks, Romans, and other peoples possessed, can not only be readily conceded, but is also certain. However, it by no means follows from this that no mysteries could have taken place among them. The Egyptians certainly had no human-like deities before they became acquainted with the Greeks, and yet they not only had mysteries, but the Greeks had even received their own from them. Even if there were no mythological stories of the gods about which one could give the *epopts* <note>original: "Epopten"; a term for those initiated into the highest grade of the mysteries</note> a better explanation, the worship of the sun, the moon, and the elements—if it is indeed true that the Persians worshipped these objects of nature—was already a sufficient subject for mysteries. This could certainly have provided sufficient cause for the establishment of **mysteries** <note>Secret religious rites or societies in antiquity, such as the Eleusinian or Mithraic mysteries.</note>. Were not the teachings regarding **demons** <note>In the Greek sense of 'daimones'—spirits or divine beings intermediate between gods and mortals.</note>, the human soul and its future state, and indeed all scientific knowledge, an equally important subject of these mysteries?

Herodotus says that the Persians worshipped **Venus Urania** <note>The 'Heavenly Venus,' representing celestial or spiritual love.</note> under the name **Mithra**, whom the Arabs called **Militta**. Meiners <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian known for his works on the history of religions.</note> maintains that the Persians, in the course of time, worshipped both the moon and the sun in human form—the latter under the name **Mithras**, specifically in the guise of a warrior in Persian clothing. All these reports are partly extremely uncertain and partly evidently incorrect. The **Militta** or **Alitta** mentioned by Herodotus was the moon, which also appears under this name in the **Koran** <note>original: "Koran"</note>. Chapter 63 <note>original: "Surat. 63"</note>. ...that they should have borrowed from the Christians. — What could that be? — Baptism? — Long before the emergence of Christianity, this type of purification <note>original: "Läuterung"</note> was already in practice in Persia, as it was in all pagan mysteries. — The offering <term>Oblation: a formal ritual offering, usually of bread or wine</term> of bread? — No one can pass this off as a type of the Lord's Supper <note>original: "Abendmahl"</note> either, since such offerings were also customary among the pagans. In the Mithraic mysteries <note>The cult of Mithras, a Roman mystery religion centered on a deity of Persian origin</note>, where the transmigration of souls <note>original: "Seelenwanderung"</note> was taught, as Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) who wrote extensively on religion</note> says, these were far more appropriate than bloody sacrifices. 

How often, however, do things possess a similarity that are anything but derived from one another. Based on such similarities, it was formerly suspected that Nestorian Christians <note>A branch of Eastern Christianity that spread along the Silk Road</note> had settled among the Tartars <note>A general historical term for various Mongolic and Turkic peoples of Central Asia</note> and influenced their religion and customs, because people believed they found the tonsure <note>The practice of shaving the hair on the scalp as a sign of religious devotion</note> of priests, anointing, and a type of Lord's Supper among them. But upon closer investigation of the matter, people have moved away from this prejudice. --- Page 222 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the origins of religious rituals; this page continues the comparison between pagan mysteries and early Christian practices.</meta>

The fact that one finds similarities in Christian customs should surprise no one: for how often did the Church Fathers <note>The influential theologians and writers of the early Christian Church</note> view everything that was customary in the pagan mysteries—which, after all, far exceeded Christianity in age—as a mockery of Christianity devised by the devil's envy and malice! According to **Porphyry** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and critic of early Christianity</note>, the tongue and hands of the initiate were smeared with honey in order to purify him from sin, and in the second, or certainly by the beginning of the third century, the baptized person was given milk and honey to taste. (*) But the usage was, as one clearly sees, very different. That Christians introduced many things from the pagan mysteries into their own practice is a completely established and well-known matter. But that the pagans adopted something from the Christians in their mysteries is something that one will likely never be able to prove with complete reliability.

# Concerning the Orphics and Pythagoreans.

Orpheus is generally regarded in the ancient world as the father of the mysteries. At the very least, he was the poet of the mysteries: for in a great many mysteries, songs of Orpheus were sung during the initiation. I shall bypass the entire investigation here into who this Orpheus actually was; Suidas <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedic lexicon of the ancient Mediterranean world.</note> names seven of them. This entire investigation borders, to a certain extent, on the realm of fable. Nevertheless, Orpheus the Thracian <note>Thrace was an ancient region in Southeast Europe, now split between Bulgaria, Greece, and Turkey; Orpheus was traditionally said to hail from there.</note> is generally regarded as the one who first introduced the

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on Mithraic mysteries and introduces Orpheus as the legendary founder and poet of Greek mystery traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Mithras, Orpheus, Orphism, Pythagoras, Greek mysteries, Suidas, Thrace, initiation rites</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of Greek religious thought, focusing specifically on the legendary figure of Orpheus and the transmission of Egyptian wisdom.</meta>

The Greeks were instructed in the knowledge of the gods and in the mysteries, and this general opinion of the ancients is further strengthened by the fact that Orphic hymns (*) were sung throughout the mysteries of the Greeks. Even in his own time, Aristotle cast doubt on whether an Orpheus had ever existed in the world. However, his doubts are not capable of overvoting the unanimous testimony of all antiquity; rather, one only sees from this that an established historical truth always loses something with the passage of time, until it finally becomes completely wavering and doubtful. The general legend of the ancients also declares that Orpheus fetched the mysteries from Egypt and brought them from there to Greece and Thrace. If one compares the Orphic doctrine with the ancient Egyptian one, this legend is uncommonly confirmed, however little it can be denied that the later philosophers <note>The author likely refers to the Neoplatonists here, who were known for attributing their own ideas to Orpheus.</note>

(*) <note>original: "PAUSANIAS Lib. I, cap. 39."</note> PAUSANIAS Book I, chapter 39. <margin>Pausanias was a 2nd-century Greek traveler and geographer whose writings are a primary source for ancient religious sites.</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the historical debate surrounding Orpheus, arguing that despite Aristotle's skepticism, the consensus of antiquity and the similarities between Egyptian and Greek mysteries confirm Orpheus as the transmitter of sacred knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, Aristotle, Pausanias, Greek Mysteries, Egyptian influence, Thrace, Orphic hymns, religious transmission</keywords>

# The Orphic Mysteries and Schools

The mysteries of the Greeks, which Orpheus brought there from Egypt, can essentially be viewed as a school of this ancient <term>mystagogue</term> <note>A teacher or initiator into religious mysteries; from the Greek 'mystagogos'.</note>. Furthermore, there were Orphics in the ancient world who are perhaps worthy of a more precise investigation—or at least deserve it just as much, and are just as closely related to the mysteries—as the Pythagoreans, of whom I shall soon speak.

Almost all the ancients who mention Orpheus make him not only the inventor of the mysteries among the Greeks and Thracians, but also the founder of a particular sect or school. In the times of Theseus <note>The mythical hero and king of Athens.</note>, this school existed among the Greeks, and it seems to have been swallowed up either by the other Greek philosophical schools, or certainly by the Pythagorean school.

As much as one can conclude from the small traces that still remain of this, they had

<summary>This page discusses the Egyptian origins of the Orphic mysteries and the establishment of an Orphic sect or school in ancient Greece prior to its absorption by the Pythagoreans.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, mysteries, Egypt, mystagogue, Orphics, Pythagoreans, Thracians, Theseus, philosophical schools</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the origins of the Orphic mysteries or the character of Orpheus himself; the catchword "But" (Aber) suggests a transition to their specific practices.</meta>

the Orphics had introduced a certain way of life among themselves, by which they distinguished themselves from their contemporaries, and which was customarily called the Orphic life. As much as one can see from Plato, they abstained from eating anything that had possessed life. They went so far in this that they did not even sacrifice animals to the gods, but rather brought them merely offerings of fruits, cakes, and honey. (*) All blood was regarded by them as a kind of defilement, whether it was consumed by humans or shed upon the altars of the gods.

Fraguier <note>Claude-François Fraguier (1666–1728), a French scholar and member of the Academy of Inscriptions</note> believes that Orpheus sought, through this law, to bring his countrymen back from the savage custom of offering human sacrifices to the gods and devouring their enemies conquered in war. (**)

(**) *Memoirs of the Paris Academy of Inscriptions*, Volume I, page 126 <note>original: "Abhandlungen der Pariser Academie der Inschriften"</note>.

<summary>This page describes the "Orphic life," a disciplined lifestyle characterized by strict vegetarianism and the rejection of blood sacrifices, which scholars suggest was intended to civilize early humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Orphics, Orpheus, Plato, Orphic life, vegetarianism, animal sacrifice, human sacrifice, Fraguier, Academy of Inscriptions</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient Greek religious practices, specifically focusing on the origins of Orphic asceticism and its connection to Egyptian philosophy.</meta>

But when one remembers that this was customary among all those peoples who had adopted the doctrine of the **transmigration of souls** <note>original: "Seelenwanderung"; also known as metempsychosis, the belief that the soul is reborn into new bodies, including animals</note>, and that Orpheus borrowed all his teachings and institutions from Egypt, where this very doctrine was accepted, one will soon discover that nothing other than the opinion on the transmigration of souls gave rise to this abstinence from all meat-eating among the Orphics.

In Euripides' *Hippolytus*, one finds an exact image of an Orphic presented in the person of Hippolytus, according to which one is able to form fairly correct concepts of what the nature of these **mystics** <term>mystics: here referring to initiates of the secret Orphic mystery religions</term> of the highest Greek antiquity must have been. "So you are," says Theseus to Hippolytus, "the superior man who has an intimate acquaintance with the gods; you are then the wise and blameless one. I will certainly not believe your vain boastings any further: the gods would..."

...therefore, because both, Orpheus and Pythagoras, had drawn from one and the same source, namely from Egyptianism <note>original: "Aegyptiacismus"; the author refers to the ancient Egyptian religious and philosophical system</note>.

Another mysterious school of the ancients is comprised of the so-called Pythagoreans. I connect them with the Orphics not only because it is certain that both borrowed their teachings and institutions from the Egyptians, but also because the Pythagoreans actually presented their doctrine as Orphic. Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote a famous biography of Pythagoras</note> not only has Pythagoras say that he had himself initiated at Libethra in Thrace by Aglaophemus; (*) but Herodotus also confirms this, and says that the Bacchics and Orphics were of one and the same origin as the Pythagoreans, namely of Egyptian origin. (**)

Heraclides Ponticus <note>A Greek philosopher and historian (c. 387–312 BC) known for his astronomical theories and for writing about the lives of famous men.</note>, his first biographer, lived only two hundred years after him, and collected his reports from oral legends. Given the great esteem in which this philosopher was held, and the human inclination to tell many wonderful things about those whom one highly values, it is therefore no wonder that much that is strange has entered into his life story. Iamblichus and Porphyry <note>Neoplatonist philosophers who wrote influential biographies of Pythagoras in the 3rd and 4th centuries AD.</note>, and all Platonic philosophers from the Alexandrian school, possessed—alongside their extraordinary esteem for Pythagoras—a great penchant for the miraculous. What is not told about Plotinus <note>The founder of Neoplatonism (c. 204–270 AD).</note> by the New Platonists! In general, the men who stood in high regard with this philosopher were nothing less than seers, the inspired, and miracle-workers. Nevertheless, one cannot cast into

# Page 197

...cast into doubt, without rejecting the general reputation of all the ancients; and when one considers where *Pythagoras* drew his knowledge, and after what model he sought to form his school, many of the more romantic elements will naturally fall away.

At the time of this philosopher, the *Egyptians* were universally famous for their extensive knowledge. The Ionian school, in which *Pythagoras* was first instructed, had its origin in *Egypt*. Through this influence, and as *Iamblichus* <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher and biographer of Pythagoras.</note> says, by *Thales* himself, *Pythagoras* was persuaded to travel to *Egypt* and to be instructed in the sciences by the priests at *Memphis* and *Thebes*. (*) On this journey, he first went to *Phoenicia*, as this same writer says, and was initiated at *Byblos* and *Tyre*. One may think of *Pythagoras* as one wishes, and one may consider him as great a visionary <note>original: "Schwärmer" – in the 18th century, this term often implied a religious enthusiast or someone prone to mystical dreaming</note> as could possibly exist in his times; nevertheless, he remains a remarkable and great man. It always requires a certain enthusiasm and a particular disposition of the human soul to be capable of undertaking such journeys and subjecting oneself to such dangers and difficulties as he did in order to attain scientific knowledge. Yet, for that reason, this entire history is not at all incredible. Anyone who might read *Anquetil’s* <note>Abraham-Hyacinthe Anquetil-Duperron (1731–1805), a French Orientalist who traveled to India to translate the Zend-Avesta</note> history a few hundred years from now would perhaps find just as much of the adventurous in it as in the history of *Pythagoras*; meanwhile, we know for a fact that all of it actually happened.

The school which *Pythagoras* established in Lower Italy bears entirely the stamp of *Egypt*. To form an order of priests, or rather a priestly lineage, upon which

This inner school was, in a sense, a type of monkhood or <term>cenobites</term> <note>From the Greek 'koinobion', meaning those living a communal life.</note>. This inner school was organized—as far as circumstances permitted—after the model of the Egyptians. Just as the external students were distinguished from the internal ones, there were again different classes among the latter. Strictly speaking, there were three. The color white was for them the image of Good, just as the color black was the image of Evil. <margin>Diogenes Laertius, Book VIII, number 19 <note>A 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers.</note></margin> Going on the hunt was entirely forbidden to them; meanwhile, they did not neglect other physical exercises, such as wrestling, running, throwing the discus <note>original: "Kugelwerfen" - literally ball-throwing, but referring to the classical athletic context</note>, and whatever else was customary among the ancients to make the body strong, and every morning and evening they took exercise by going for walks. <meta>The previous page discussed the dietary restrictions and initial disciplines of the Pythagorean school. This page continues the description of their daily lifestyle and social isolation.</meta>

Pythagoras eventually departed from the aforementioned discipline <note>referring to the strict Egyptian prohibition of wine</note>, perhaps because he knew that this ban among the Egyptians had actually been purely political; nevertheless, the Pythagoreans only drank wine in the evenings during their meals. They made use of the bath daily: however, they were not permitted to go into a public bathhouse where others had bathed, so that they would not be contaminated. One sees from these traits that Pythagoras was consistently intent on placing his students outside of all connection with other people. This even extended to their religious worship, in that they took no part at all in bloody sacrifices <note>animal sacrifices, which were standard in Greek civic religion</note>.

This was the appearance of the strange society that Pythagoras established. It is perfectly similar to a monastic order, and I believe that one does not do entirely wrong if one calls the Pythagoreans the **Carthusians of Paganism** <note>The Carthusians are a Catholic monastic order known for extreme silence and isolation; the author uses this to explain the Pythagorean lifestyle to an 18th-century audience</note>. # On the Pythagorean Society

In all times, there have been people whose intellect has taken a peculiar direction. Pythagoras had the model of the Egyptians before him, which he sought to copy exactly; and since religion, the state constitution, and many other things prevented him from making his image perfectly identical to the original, his society could naturally take on no other form.

His instruction, which he imparted to his listeners, was—as I have already noted—symbolic. This even extended to the moral precepts he gave, of which Diogenes Laertius, Iamblichus, and Porphyry <note>These are three major ancient biographers of Pythagoras; their accounts are the primary sources for his life and philosophy.</note> have preserved various examples for us. The inner doctrine of the Pythagoreans, however, was fundamentally the same as what was presented in the Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refers to secret religious rites of ancient Greece, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were only open to initiated members.</note>. The theology of the Pythagoreans was just as much an antithesis <note>original: "Antithese"</note> to the popular religion as that which was presented in the Mysteries: for Pythagoras likewise taught the existence of a supreme being, which he called sometimes the Monad <note>original: "Monas"; a term referring to the "One" or the first principle of all things</note>, sometimes

<summary>This page examines how Pythagoras modeled his secret society after Egyptian traditions and explains that his "inner doctrine" served as a philosophical alternative to the common public religion of his time.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Egyptian influence, secret societies, ancient philosophy, Monad, Greek Mysteries, Diogenes Laertius, Iamblichus, Porphyry</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the foundational principles of Pythagoras's school; this page continues by examining the eclectic sources of his mystical teachings and his influence on later Neoplatonists like Iamblichus.</meta>

which he called sometimes the *Tetras* <note>The number four, representing the source of eternal nature</note>, and other times the *Tetractys*, and his own students say that in his teachings regarding the gods, he borrowed some things from the Orphics <note>Followers of the mythical poet Orpheus</note>, some from the Egyptian priests, from the Chaldeans, the Magi, and from the Eleusinian and Samothracian mysteries. (*) From this, one can already conclude what kind of doctrines were presented here. Alongside these, he recommended the veneration of the gods and the heroes. This provides the key to his entire doctrine of demons <note>In the Greek sense of 'daimones' or intermediary spirits, not necessarily evil beings</note>, and *Iamblichus* says of him exactly what was otherwise usually said of the great Eleusinian mysteries: namely, that through his instruction, one arrives at precise and correct concepts of the gods. All Pythagoreans were also friends of theory and theurgy <note>The practice of rituals intended to invoke the presence of gods or spirits</note>, and they primarily practiced various types of *divinations* <note>original: "Divinationen"</note>. (**) Furthermore, the Pythagoreans applied themselves

The Pythagoreans also devoted themselves just as much to *medicine*. (*) In the works of *Porphyry* and *Iamblichus*, one finds various highly fabulous tales of the miracles of *Pythagoras*, entirely in the style of the Alexandrian Platonists. accused of? Naudé has undertaken his defense. (*) <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653) was a French scholar and librarian who wrote "Apologie pour tous les grands personnages qui ont esté faussement soupçonnez de magie," defending famous figures against charges of magic.</note>

As much strange material as one encounters regarding the Pythagorean school, it is nonetheless always to be regretted that no written essays from it have come down to us. Meanwhile, Plato did nonetheless purchase some writings of the Pythagoreans. But of all these, almost no reliable traces remain today.

### XI.
## Concerning the Hidden Method of Teaching.

Pythagoras presented his teachings through symbols <note>The German "Bildern" here refers to metaphorical or symbolic representations used to conceal deeper meanings.</note>, and whatever else was written by the Pythagoreans was likewise in a figurative

<margin>(*) Naudé, Defense of Great Men. Chapter XV. p. 215.</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the lack of surviving written records from the Pythagorean school due to its tradition of oral secrecy and introduces Pythagoras's use of symbolic or "hidden" teaching methods.</summary>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on how ancient wisdom was presented; this page continues that thought by explaining the hidden methods of the Pythagoreans and Egyptians.</meta>

and presented in a hidden manner. In this regard, Pythagoras was—as in so many other things—a precise follower of the Egyptians. The very same respect that the Egyptians, and indeed all peoples who possessed Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refer to secret religious rites or societies of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, where sacred knowledge was revealed only to initiated members.</note>, held toward the secrets contained within them, also moved them to withdraw this knowledge from the eyes of strangers by hiding it under symbols or images that were intelligible only to the initiated. And here, the hieroglyphs of the Egyptians rightly take the first place.

<margin>(*) Kraft’s *Customs of the Savages*. p. 200. ff. <note>Reference to Friedrich Wilhelm Kraft (1712–1758), a German theologian and author.</note></margin>
The actual origin of hieroglyphs undoubtedly falls within those times when the sciences were still entirely in their infancy; when letters had not yet been invented, but rather the object one wished to express was painted in its entirety. Similar hieroglyphs are still to be found today among some wild peoples in America, (*) and these representations

<summary>This page discusses how Pythagoras adopted the Egyptian practice of using symbols and hieroglyphs to conceal sacred knowledge from the uninitiated, linking the origin of hieroglyphs to early pictorial communication.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the origins of writing systems or early symbolic communication, leading into this comparison with Egyptian hieroglyphs.</meta>

are so similar to the hieroglyphs of the Egyptians,
that one might say they were copied from one another.
This style of writing can, by its very nature,
only last as long as the sciences
are still in their most delicate infancy, and so
long as one deals only with sensory objects
that can be painted. It must,
however, lose its usefulness as soon as the
sciences rise even slightly, and one
also has to deal with things that do not
fall within the senses. To be sure, one then
takes refuge in allegory for a while,
in order to represent entirely different objects
under foreign images by means of a certain
similarity and correspondence. But this
also cannot hold out for long. One must finally
abandon pictorial writing <note>original: "Bilderschrift"</note> as inadequate,
and reach for other means to express everything
that the sciences offer upon some
expansion.

But it is a different question whether the hieroglyphs <note>The term "hieroglyph" literally translates from Greek as "sacred carving."</note> were invented by the Egyptians? This question depends on whether the Egyptians arrived in Egypt as an already cultivated people, or whether they were a people who worked their way up from a state of savagery to culture? If the latter is true, then the hieroglyphs were invented in Egypt. If the former is true, then they brought them with them into Egypt, and perhaps even then they were already the scholarly script used for monuments and sacred writings. This latter view seems the most probable to me; but I cannot elaborate on this matter here.

In the time of Herodotus <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BCE) was a Greek historian often called the "Father of History."</note>, these two types of characters were known in Egypt. They use, he says, two kinds of letters; some are called the sacred, but the others are called the common. (*)

But it is remarkable that Herodotus <note>Greek historian, 5th century BCE</note> does not provide an explanation for even a single monument written in the sacred character. Warburton <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop who wrote extensively on the origin of language and hieroglyphs</note>, who otherwise has treated the hieroglyphs of the Egyptians most capably, derives four distinct types of writing from what Porphyry and Clement of Alexandria <note>Early Christian and Neoplatonist scholars who wrote about Egyptian culture</note> say regarding the hieroglyphs of the Egyptians, namely:

1) The actual hieroglyphs, which represented objects in pictures exactly as they were.
2) The symbolic style of writing, where invisible objects were expressed through sensory ones according to a certain similarity and comparison.
3) The alphabetical script, which he calls the epistolary character <note>original: "epistolischen Charakter"; today commonly known as Demotic</note>, and
4) Yet another secret cipher of the priests, which he calls the hierogrammatic style of writing <note>original: "hierogrammatische Schreibart"</note>.

But, if one examines the matter closely, this is entirely incorrect. The so-called symbolic

<summary>The author critiques Herodotus's lack of hieroglyphic knowledge and disputes William Warburton's four-part classification of Egyptian writing systems.</summary>
<keywords>Herodotus, hieroglyphs, William Warburton, Porphyry, Clement of Alexandria, Egyptian writing, symbolic writing, epistolary character, hierogrammatic script</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the origins of ancient writing systems; this page continues the analysis of Egyptian scripts and critiques historical classifications.</meta>

The symbolic style of writing was not at all distinct from the hieroglyphs themselves. It was actually nothing more than a completion of those inadequate signs, which, with the further expansion of the sciences, were not sufficient to express everything. New images were used to express non-sensory objects by means of similarity. Porphyry <note>Porphyry (c. 234–305 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on the life of Pythagoras and ancient symbolism.</note> does indeed make a distinction between the two (\*); but it is visible that he errs in this, and he does not mention the fourth category at all, namely the secret cipher. Clement of Alexandria <note>Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150–215 AD), a Christian theologian who provided one of the few detailed ancient accounts of Egyptian writing.</note> is actually the only one who mentions a fourfold style of writing (\*\*). But it is clear that he was mistaken, and took the so-called priestly character <note>term: Hieratic or Demotic script, often associated with the priesthood</note> for a distinct style of writing, just as he divides the hieroglyphic style itself into the "kyriological" <note>original: "Kyriologische" - from the Greek 'kyrios' (proper/dominant), referring to literal or phonetic representation</note>, which paints objects as they are, and the "enigmatic" <note>original: "änigmatische" - referring to allegorical or symbolic meanings</note>.

The preservation of the ancient character in their scientific essays eventually became a formal study among the Egyptians, and whoever among them desired to possess an accurate and complete knowledge of the mysteries first had to learn these characters. Furthermore, it is undeniable that the Egyptians, in their presentation of the mysteries, still made use of images; just as they wrote with hieroglyphs, they also shrouded their teachings in riddles. They possessed not only figurative expressions behind which they concealed the truth, but also entire fables, or so-called <term>sacred discourses <note>original: "ἱερους λογους" (hierous logous)</note></term>, which contained entirely different matters than they promised by outward appearance. Of the alleged works of Orpheus, his Hymns, his *Argonautica*, his poems on stones <note>The *Lithica*, a poem about the magical properties of gemstones</note>, and several fragments still remain today. # On the Authorship of Ancient Works

There were many Pythagoreans who placed their own names upon their works (*). For this reason, Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a renowned classical scholar and bibliographer</note> has already expressed doubt as to whether Pythagoras himself left behind any written essays (**). Orpheus was, as I have already stated, in the truest sense the poet of the mysteries, and his songs were even preferred over those of Homer, although the latter possessed greater beauty. It is therefore very probable that in subsequent times, the name of Orpheus was also attached to other poems and hymns if they were sung during the mysteries or dealt with the secrets themselves, even if they did not actually originate from this ancient <term>mystagogue</term> <note>A priest or initiator into sacred mysteries</note>.

(*) IAMBLICHUS, *On the Life of Pythagoras*, p. 164. <note>original: "de Vita Pythag."</note>
(**) FABRICIUS, *Greek Library*, Book II, chapter 12. <note>original: "Bibl. graec. Lib. II. c. 12."</note>

# XII.

## On the Decline of the Mysteries.

That mysteries existed in the ancient world, and that they were held in general high esteem, is evident from what has preceded. Their subject matter was indeed most carefully concealed from all strangers; but it could not remain so hidden that a word or two did not escape here and there. These individual words have been collected, a certain whole has been composed from them, and from this one finally sees that their content was important, and that they were generally not unworthy of the respect which so many great men of antiquity, and almost the entire pagan world, harbored toward them. What now remains is the question: where have they gone? When did they expire, and what gave the actual occasion for their end?

<sig>P 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: One</meta>

<note>The author uses the term "Mysterien" to refer to the secret religious rites of ancient Greece and Rome, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter XII, transitioning from the historical importance and secrecy of ancient mysteries to an inquiry into the causes and timing of their eventual decline.</summary>
<keywords>ancient mysteries, pagan world, historical decline, secrecy, religious rites</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
One can settle the matter very briefly if one simply answers that they perished as pagan mysteries when the Christian religion became dominant in the Roman Empire. But that this answer is anything but sufficient, and does not apply to all specific types of mysteries, requires no proof. It is, rather, certain that some mysteries maintained themselves long after the times when Christianity was already the dominant religion, and only after that time—uncertain by what accident—lost themselves from the world, so that now not even the slightest trace of them can be found. This deserves a somewhat more precise investigation, which perhaps will not be entirely unpleasant to my readers: an investigation that, even now, can be of much use if properly applied.

Of some mysteries, one can state the date of their downfall with fair certainty. The Egyptian mysteries suffered a significant disruption with the decline of the

<summary>The author argues against the simplistic view that pagan mysteries vanished immediately upon the rise of Christianity, suggesting instead that many persisted for centuries and deserve closer historical investigation.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the Persian conquest of Egypt and the initial impact on local customs. The catchword "tra:" suggests a transition to a discussion on the "tradition" or "transfer" of these mysteries.</meta>

It is difficult to understand how a ruler of the Persians, who were the declared enemies of idolatry <note>The Persians, following Zoroastrianism, generally rejected the use of cult statues or "idols" common in other ancient religions.</note>, could support Egyptian superstition to such an extent that—as *Polyaenus* says (*)—he promised one hundred talents to anyone who could produce a new *Apis* <note>The Apis was a sacred bull worshipped in Memphis, seen as an incarnation of the god Ptah.</note>. Under the rule of the Persians, the Egyptians experienced alternating fortunes; if they ever had a harsh sovereign, he was followed by another under whom they fared better, and their entire religion remained undisturbed. Their mysteries, likewise, suffered no harm.

Jablonsky <note>Paul Ernst Jablonski (1693–1757), a famous scholar of Coptic and Egyptian mythology.</note> believes that the internal wars which troubled Egypt under Persian rule caused the decline of the mysteries. Specifically, he argues that the Egyptians were thereby prevented from undergoing the difficult and lengthy trials that necessarily preceded initiation into the mysteries (**). I would have no hesitation—

In all probability, the genuine Egyptian mysteries were carried to their grave, or rather, they donned the clothing of the Greeks; and under this new garment, the old disappeared to such an extent that finally very few traces of it remained. When Strabo <note>A Greek geographer and philosopher (c. 64 BC – 24 AD) who traveled extensively throughout the Roman Empire.</note> stayed in Egypt during the time of Augustus, there was no longer any <term>Hierogrammateus</term> <note>original: "Hierogrammateus"; a sacred scribe or keeper of the holy records</note> present, who had formerly been the possessors of the secrets; instead, in Heliopolis <note>One of the oldest cities in ancient Egypt, a major religious center for the sun god Ra.</note>, they only showed the houses in which the priests had formerly had their seat. (*) What was still present at that time were, in a sense, only the lowest levels of the mysteries: those greater ones had long since expired with that generation which, for so many centuries, had believed itself to be in exclusive possession of them.

The school of the Orphics <note>Followers of Orphism, a set of religious beliefs and practices associated with the mythical poet Orpheus.</note> does not seem to have maintained itself for very long among the Greeks. Here, certainly, that which occurred did not take place...

<meta>The previous page discussed the origins of the Orphic mysteries; this page continues with the decline of that school and its transition into Pythagoreanism. Catchword "al" likely refers to "allein" or "allerdings" on the following page.</meta>

The downfall of the *Egyptian* school was accelerated, but other causes were present which made it impossible for such a *society* to have maintained itself for long. The *Orphic* life—the abstinence from all living creatures <note>This refers to Orphic vegetarianism, rooted in the belief in the transmigration of souls</note>—created too strong a *contrast* with the *Greek religion* and the customs of the *Greeks*. Among them, such severity was something so unusual that a society of such eccentrics could hardly have expected a large increase in membership. Perhaps the servants of the folk *religion* <note>The established priesthoods of the Greek city-states</note> also contributed their part to suppress people for whom the highest level of holiness was exactly what could be viewed by the priests as *irreligion*—namely, the abstinence from bloody sacrifices. Finally, this school lost itself among the *Pythagoreans*, who essentially wished to be regarded as nothing other than the successors of the *Orphics*.

The school of *Pythagoras* spread with great speed throughout lower *Italy* <note>The region known as Magna Graecia</note>

Some believe that it met its downfall during the times of Pythagoras himself, and that this philosopher was himself buried in the ruin of his <term>sect<note>In this historical context, "sect" refers to a philosophical school or organized following rather than a modern religious splinter group.</note></term>. The occasion for the downfall of the Pythagoreans is reported in various ways. Iamblichus relates that a certain Cylon, a wealthy and distinguished man among the Crotoniates, but who possessed a poor moral character, had endeavored to be accepted into the society of the Pythagoreans, but received a negative answer, and afterwards, out of resentment, sought to bring the Pythagoreans to ruin. # The Transition of the Mysteries

When Christianity began to challenge Paganism <note>original: "Heidentum"</note>, the Alexandrian school <note>The Neoplatonic school of Alexandria, which sought to reconcile Greek philosophy with spiritual mysticism</note> particularly took up the cause of the declining Pagan faith. If one knows the principles of this school precisely, and is even somewhat informed about what occurred and was discussed within the Mysteries <term>Mysteries: secret religious rites of ancient Greece and Rome, such as those at Eleusis, accessible only to initiates</term>, then one will not long remain in doubt that both drew from one and the same source.

This school was also the place of refuge for the Mysteries when harsh imperial edicts would no longer allow them any freedom; in essence, it was nothing other than the Mysteries themselves, but under the mask of philosophy. The so-called "Lesser Mysteries" certainly came to an end through the decrees made by the Emperor Theodosius <note>Theodosius I (reigned 379–395 AD), who issued decrees effectively making Nicene Christianity the state religion and banning pagan rituals</note>; but the "Greater Mysteries" continued on among the philosophers of the Alexandrian school. They presumably only ceased to exist from the year 528 onwards, when the philosophers of this school—as the strongest support of Paganism—

<summary>This page argues that the ancient Greek Mysteries did not disappear under Christian pressure but survived by transforming into the philosophical teachings of the Alexandrian school until the 6th century.</summary>
<keywords>Alexandrian school, Neoplatonism, Paganism, Christian history, Emperor Theodosius, Greater Mysteries, religious transition</keywords>

# Concepts of the Origin and Purpose of this Society

The concepts that the Abbot R... <note>Abbé Claude-Christophe-Alain Robin (1750–1794), a French priest and writer on Freemasonry</note> has formed regarding the origin of the constitution and the purpose of this Society (*) compel me to examine this theory with some precision—insofar as it can be done before the eyes of the world—and to provide concepts that are as correct and consistent with the truth as I have previously endeavored to defend the Order against its adversaries.

The first question that arises here is this: whether at all, and to what extent, **Masonry** <term>Maurerey: an older term for Freemasonry</term> can be placed in parallel with the **Mysteries of the Ancients**? <note>Refers to the secret religious rites of Greece and Rome, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries</note> This question cannot be answered other than in very different ways, depending on the perspective taken. It is a fundamental law among the Freemasons that their society contains nothing that would be contrary to religion, the state, or good morals. I well believe that among the so-called Freemasons there are enough people who—
