Here we immediately find an important trait that distinguishes the ancient mysteries from those of the **Freemasonic Order** in a very striking way. One would, of course, act very unfairly if one were to say of the mysteries of the ancients that they were against the state, or against religion and morality. The truths that were presented in them were, in fact, true religion. The state itself protected them and made use of the actual "Lesser Mysteries" <note>The Lesser Mysteries (mikra mysteria) were the preliminary initiations held at Agrae, serving as a prerequisite for the Greater Mysteries.</note> as an important support for the general welfare; and however corrupt the morals became in some mysteries over time, these were nevertheless degenerations. But it remains certain that these mysteries, if they had been made public, would have completely overturned not only the entire prevailing religion but also the state itself. One can therefore maintain of them—regardless of their truth and inner goodness—that they were opposed to the religion and the state, and it was only...

Just as the authentic secrets of Masonry in their outward constitution are contrary neither to religion, nor to the state, nor to morality, so too are they not so in their inner essence. There no longer exists today an external religion for the common people and an internal mystery-religion, as was the case in the pagan world; and from this alone one sees the important and essential difference that prevails between those ancient mysteries and these.

Another important difference lies in the character of the persons to whom the mysteries were communicated. Among the Egyptians and other ancient peoples, they were bound to a certain sacred tribe; and although they were somewhat more open among the Greeks, they were nevertheless always the portion of the priesthood, or of such persons who held the highest offices in the state. The nature of <term>mysteriocrypsy</term> <note>The practice of concealing mysteries or keeping secrets hidden from the uninitiated.</note> provides yet another important distinction between the ancient and modern mysteries. The primary reason why the mysteries were withdrawn from the world's view among the ancients was to avoid the detriment that could accrue to the state and the prevailing religion through their discovery. None of this applies to <term>Masonry</term> <note>Freemasonry.</note>. Were it possible for it to be discovered and presented to the world, one would see with admiration that it has no bearing on the state or religion, and therefore, from this perspective, it could certainly be presented to the world. And if there were indeed an influence that it had on one or the other, it would certainly not be one that could be detrimental to their peace and happiness.

The individuals to whom these secrets were communicated, and the reasons for their concealment, differ greatly; they also diverge just as widely regarding what one expects to gain from them. From the lesser secrets, one expected a better condition in the future world, and from the greater secrets, nothing less than <term>Theosis<note>original: "Θεωσις"; the process of becoming divine or achieving union with God.</note></term>. All of these are things entirely unknown to Freemasonry. It is well known that the future state of mankind depends not on initiations and external ceremonies, but solely on moral improvement. One would, therefore, stray very far from the essence of the Order if one wished to offer its members such prospects. The same can also be said with justice regarding the origin of the ancient mysteries and Freemasonry. However, I shall deal with this specifically hereafter.

On the other hand, a great **agreement** is found between them from another perspective. This lies not so much in their mutual relationship, or in the fact that the latter were formed and arranged according to the pattern of the former: but rather, if *mysteries* <note>The author refers to the "Mysteries" of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian or Orphic rites.</note> were founded even today that departed as far from both as *Masonry* <note>Freemasonry</note> does from the ancient *mysteries*, I am certain that in exactly the same respects such an **agreement** would take place. This lies in the nature of the things themselves.

# On the Exclusion of Women and the Gradual Revelation of Knowledge

The concern was undoubtedly that from the union of both sexes, either actual disorders might arise, or that outsiders might take occasion from it to form detrimental ideas about the secrets. If women were excluded from some mysteries of the ancients, men were, on the contrary, excluded from others. Therefore, in order not to only avoid actual disorders, but also to give outsiders no opportunity to think detrimentally of the Order, access to Masonry is entirely denied to women. Furthermore, if the secrets of the ancient world—both in their inner essence and in the external customs associated with them—were of such a nature that the other sex could not conveniently be receptive to them, then this likewise applies to Masonry. <note>The author reflects the 18th-century view that Freemasonry, like certain ancient rites, was gender-specific to maintain social reputation and because the rituals were deemed "unsuitable" for women.</note>

Another correspondence is found in the manner of communication itself. All human knowledge is gradual. This is the human—

It is inherent to human nature, which is not made

mysteries among the ancients were arranged in this way.
Symbolic knowledge was the first thing

Among the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Pythagoras, known for their secrecy and mathematical mysticism.</note>, it was riddles
and mathematical figures to which one was initially
introduced. These were, as it were, the images beneath which the deeply hidden truth
lay concealed. One had to first become acquainted with these,
and in such a way, one had to accustom the inner eye,
which would undoubtedly have been blinded by a sudden and
completely unobstructed view. The mysteries of

nature. They are too great to be presented immediately

--- Page 302 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the origins of secret societies; this page continues by examining the psychological wisdom of the founders of Freemasonry.</meta>

The founders of the <term>Masonic secrets</term><note>original: "maureriſchen Geheimniſſe"; refers to the rituals and hidden knowledge of Freemasonry</note> were, as one perceives on countless occasions, great connoisseurs of the human heart. As such, they chose the same path in communicating their secrets as that which had already been trodden in the ancient world. They did not only secure these sublime secrets from the curiosity of strangers through the mystical veils in which they were wrapped, presenting them in such a manner to the still unaccustomed eyes of the <term>initiates</term><note>original: "Geweihten"; those who have been formally admitted into the secret rites</note>; but they also deemed it necessary to lead the <term>Sons of the Order</term><note>original: "Söhne des Ordens"; a traditional fraternal designation for members of the lodge</note> toward the full view only gradually. This was done so that they might enjoy the benefits more thoroughly and perfectly which the Order was intended to grant them, and which they otherwise would not have been capable of grasping in their full extent. How beautiful it is when everything unfolds at the end, and at the goal one can look back over the entire path traveled, seeing then with joy and admiration that nothing was in vain, and nothing was for naught.

<summary>This page discusses the pedagogical and psychological strategy of the Masonic founders, who used gradual initiation and "mystical veils" to ensure members fully understood the Order's secrets. It emphasizes that the slow revelation of knowledge was modeled after ancient mystery schools to prevent overwhelming the initiates.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, initiation, secrecy, psychology of ritual, Sons of the Order, ancient mysteries, gradual revelation</keywords>

In the ancient world, one did not arrive at a share in the great secrets except after many arduous trials, and even the initiation into the lesser mysteries was connected with similar customs. Some of these were arranged solely with the intention that they should be tests, or perhaps to hold back those who wished to approach the secrets. Of this kind were the trials to which Pythagoras <note>The famous Greek philosopher and mathematician (c. 570–495 BC) who was said to have traveled to Egypt to study with their priests.</note> had to submit himself among the Egyptian priests. Others, on the other hand, as far as one can gather, were either symbolic or had physical causes as their basis. Access to the secrets of Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey" — referring here to Freemasonry.</note> likewise has preceding trials, and even if these are very different from those of the ancient mysteries, the path is nevertheless not without difficulties. In a certain respect, one can say that the entire life of a Mason, until he reaches happy completion, is a time of trial, and the O... <note>The text cuts off here due to the binding; likely "Orden" (Order) or "Ort" (Place).</note>

One would certainly act very imprudently, and would not know how to properly value the high worth of that which he keeps locked within his inner self, if he were to entrust the same to everyone without distinction, without having first perfectly convinced himself that he is entrusting his secret to worthy and reliable people.

Another important correspondence is found between the ancient and modern mysteries <note>The author is comparing ancient Greco-Roman mystery cults with contemporary Freemasonry</note> when one compares their respective ultimate purposes. The subject matter in both is admittedly very different from one another, as must soon become visible to anyone who is even slightly acquainted with the secrets of the ancients. It belongs to the first primary and fundamental principles of Masonry <term>Maurerey: Freemasonry</term> that its secrets contain nothing that comes into any collision with religion, regardless of which Christian party or church may be the dominant one in the state. The great mysteries of the ancients, on the other hand, were—aside from some scientific knowledge—the direct antithesis <note>original: "Antithese"</note> of the

But nevertheless, a very precise parallel can be drawn between the secrets of Masonry and the secrets of the Ancients <note>The "Ancients" refers to the practitioners of the Greco-Roman Mystery cults, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries</note> regarding their purpose. The purpose of the Lesser Mysteries was connected with the state; the purpose of the Greater Mysteries, however, was not at all: it concerned only those who had been fortunate enough to obtain access to them. It consisted solely in preserving the true doctrine—which one could not, or would not, or should not speak of publicly—entrusting it to a worthy posterity, and in such a way snatching it from total destruction: and certainly those who were initiated into them must have considered themselves uncommonly fortunate above all other men who stood beside them. It does not surprise me at all that the Ancients judged their secrets so uncommonly favorably, since they did indeed grant them so very much. It is exactly the same with the purpose of the secrets of the Freemasonic

# On the Nature of the Order

The entire nature and character of the <term>Order</term><note>The Freemasons often referred to their organization simply as "the Order."</note> does not permit its secrets to be made known to the world. However, to preserve that which they grant from destruction, and to hand it down to a worthy posterity just as it was received—that is the purpose of the <term>Freemasons</term><note>original: "Freymaurer"</note>. This purpose can therefore only extend to those who belong to this Order, and these members must also, by their very nature, feel the great happiness that has fallen to their lot in such a way, ahead of so many of their fellow men. It can therefore surprise no one if a Freemason seeks to portray his Order in the most favorable light.

With this, the objections also fall away which an anonymous author recently made against <term>Freemasonry</term><note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> in a manner that is very shameful for the author himself. <margin>(*) Thoughts on Freemasonry.</margin> For if this is the purpose of the Order—and I know of no other—how then can one wonder that

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the secrecy of Freemasonry is necessary for the preservation of its traditions for future generations and dismisses anonymous critics of the fraternity.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, secrecy, tradition, preservation, defense of the order, anonymous critics</keywords>

--- Page 307 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical defense of Freemasonry, specifically addressing the nature of the "secret" and how it compares to ancient mystery schools.</meta>

<page-num>272</page-num>
<sig>XIV.</sig>

...that nothing becomes known of the intentions which the Masons <note>original: "Maurer"</note> seek to achieve? How can one declare their secret to be a non-entity simply because it does not extend itself, with its consequences and effects, over the entire world? If the secret is a good <note>or "asset/benefit"</note> that is bound only to the society <note>original: "Societät"</note>; then it cannot act further than within this society. If the preservation and propagation of this good is the purpose of the Order; then nothing else of it can become visible to the public, other than this one thing: that the Freemasons seek to preserve themselves.

A connoisseur of ancient and modern secrets <note>The author likely refers to the "Ancient Mysteries" of Egypt or Greece versus the "Modern Mysteries" of the Enlightenment</note> will undoubtedly be able to collect several more individual traits in which both agree with one another, and again diverge from one another. What has been said here, however, will already be sufficient to give some general concepts of the new mysteries <note>original: "Mysterien"</note> in general.

---

# XIV.

## On the Origin of the Free-
## mason Order.

Before I say anything on this matter, I must immediately remind the perhaps curious reader in advance that I am not in a position to provide the information regarding this which he, in all probability, expects or might even demand of me. I have said it once before: a historical fact <note>original: "historisches Factum"</note> cannot possibly constitute the secret of the Freemasons. There are, to be sure, ignorant—or if one prefers, false—Freemasons who view a history as something essential and very mysterious, and whose entire knowledge actually consists only of storytelling. But such people do not deserve the slightest respect. Meanwhile, very little can be said to the world about the origin of the Order <term>Order: the organized society of Freemasonry</term>, precisely because

This opinion alone has given occasion for the **Freemasons** <term>Freemasons: a fraternal organization that traces its origins to the local guilds of stonemasons</term> to generally derive their origin from England. But besides the fact that Cromwell's principles, plan, and entire behavior are essentially quite contrary to Masonry—and thus it is impossible for them to have had him as their founder—the inaccuracy of this hypothesis is also evident from the fact that Freemasons existed in Great Britain long before Cromwell's time, and that the Order even had a great adversary in this **Usurper** <note>original: "Usurpateur"; a derogatory term for Cromwell, implying he took power illegally</note>. Even if the Order were nothing other than a continuation of the old <term>Chevalerie<note>French for Knighthood or Chivalry; the author is referencing the theory that Freemasonry descended directly from medieval knights.</note></term>, as the author believes, then St. George would likely have a better claim to the patronage of <term>Masonry<note>German: Maurerey; an older term for Freemasonry.</note></term> than St. John. However, if the <term>Freemasons<note>German: Freymaurer.</note></term> regard the Feast of St. John as a day particularly holy to them, it does not happen because they view the Forerunner of the Savior <note>John the Baptist.</note> as their patron saint, but because this day is very remarkable for them in many respects; and the celebration of the same is not a festival dedicated to a patron saint, but rather a joyful remembrance of certain great benefits that flowed to the Order on this day.

Yet, the comparisons made by the author between the old Knighthood and Masonry—in order to derive the latter from the former—are consistently of this nature. They are a testament to the <term>wit<note>German: Wiz (modern Witz); here meaning intellectual ingenuity or cleverness rather than humor.</note></term> of the author and his ability to seek out similarities. But they are also a proof of how

<summary>The text critiques the theory that Freemasonry originated from medieval chivalry, arguing that the celebration of St. John's Day is based on historical milestones of the Order rather than religious patronage.</summary>

It is incredibly easy to lose one's way when one gives free rein to mere wit. I cannot possibly let one observation pass here, and this concerns the important question: what purpose does the whole of <term>Masonry</term> <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> serve if it is nothing other than a continuation of the old <term>Chivalry</term> <note>original: "Chevalerie"; the medieval knightly system with its religious, moral, and social code</note>? If this is true, then I cannot help but consider it a completely aimless, ridiculous, and highly superfluous <term>society</term> <note>original: "Societät"</note> within the state. A <term>knight</term> <note>original: "Ritter"</note>, however well he may have carried himself in all his finery in those ancient times, and however much good may have been established thereby in those dark and barbaric ages, is nevertheless a ridiculous figure in our century.

If someone plays this role for himself alone, one rightly fears a false path of the soul <note>The author suggests that clinging to outdated chivalric fantasies is a sign of mental instability or "derangement."</note>. The entire constitution of the world has completely overturned since those times, and to imagine that ancient knighthood <term>Ritterschaft: the medieval system of chivalry and knightly orders</term> within Masonry means imagining a thing that cannot exist at all with our present constitution of the world. It fits as little into our modern world as a Roman legion with bows, arrows, and battle-axes would fit into a single one of our modern armies: and the continuation of such a society <note>original: "Societät"</note> would, closely considered, be nothing other than the rearing of a monstrosity. — But how aimless, and therefore how superfluous and foolish, the entire Masonic Order <term>Maurerorden: the organized body of Freemasonry</term> would be under this hypothesis. I already mentioned at the very beginning of this section that I am not in a position to satisfy the curiosity of the foreign reader with a detailed and precise account of the origin of Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"; the author uses this term interchangeably with Freemasonry</note>. I will, however, attempt to shed some light on this subject. One does not judge entirely incorrectly when one derives Masonry from Britain. It is from there, at least, that Freemasonry came to all other European nations, and has spread with trade into the most remote parts of the world, so much so that one would find very few European settlements in other parts of the globe where Freemasons would not also be encountered. <meta>The text continues the discussion regarding the historical origins of Freemasonry, specifically critiquing popular myths of its antiquity.</meta>

Mr. de la Lande <note>Joseph Jérôme Lefrançois de Lalande (1732–1807), a famous French astronomer and prominent Freemason who wrote the article on Masonry in the French Encyclopedia.</note> may be a member of ever so many Academies of Sciences, yet this account of the origin of the Freemasons in the *Encyclopedia* is anything but well-founded. It is just as impossible to think of Masonry existing under Ingo, King of Sweden, around the year 1125, or under Alexander III in Scotland around the year 1284. It is of more recent origin, and one need only be somewhat familiar with the external and internal nature of this society to realize that certain claims regarding the great age of this society must be regarded as nothing more than empty and entirely groundless boasting.

Notwithstanding this, it also cannot be denied that the Order, when viewed from another perspective, is uncommonly old. If age gives things a superior value, then one would likely find few things that could be compared even in some small measure with this Order and its secrets; and King Edwin and Athelstan <note>Athelstan (reigned 924–939) and his son or brother Edwin are legendary figures in Masonic "Old Charges," credited with granting the first royal charter to Masons at York.</note> still live in many

in youthful times. But even here, things often become confused; when one hears of the great age of the Order, one seeks to specify its origin at a point where, by the very nature of things, it is impossible to find even the slightest trace of it.

Truth and virtue are as old as the world, and have always had admirers, worshippers, and warm friends among mankind. Just as it is essentially characteristic of human nature—while still uncorrupted—to have a feeling for physical beauty, the same applies to moral beauty; no wicked person is so depraved that they should not admire the good, even if it is far removed from them. ### XV.

## On the Great Number of
## Freemasons.

Nothing creates a greater contrast with the magnitude of Masonic secrets than the very large number of Freemasons found in the world. In Germany alone, according to the lowest estimate, over twenty thousand Freemasons are to be found. How great is their number in the remaining European states, and if one adds to that all those who

<summary>The author introduces Chapter XV, addressing the apparent contradiction between the "secrecy" of Freemasonry and the vast number of its members across Europe.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, secrecy, membership statistics, Germany, public perception</keywords>

--- Page 343 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the global spread of the fraternity; this page continues by estimating the total number of members worldwide.</meta>

located in the other parts of the world; thus, there has certainly never been an order in the world that has equaled the **Masonic Order** <note>original: "Maurer-Orden"</note> in the multitude of its members. One million people is the very least one can assume belong to the Order. (*)

---

(*) I set here the very lowest number. The author of the *Religious Events* <note>original: "Religionsbegebenheiten," likely referring to a contemporary periodical or book series documenting religious and social movements</note> puts the number of **Freemasons** at 2 million. It is incomprehensible where he obtained this information? For among all Freemasons in the world, it was only about 16 years ago that some German Freemasons first came upon the idea of recording lists of all members belonging to their particular party <note>original: "Partey," here meaning a specific branch or rite of Masonry</note>. But this undertaking was never properly carried out, and finally was almost entirely abandoned. Others have never even thought of it. It is therefore an absolute impossibility to determine how numerous the Order is. If the other reports by the author of the *Religious Events* regarding the Freemasons are not derived from better sources, then one can easily see what judgment should be passed upon them. If only people would not speak of things they do not know.

Even in England, Masonry has always been very widespread, and it was no sooner established there than it already gained a large number of members. One can deduce this from the many lodges that were established in London alone, some of which are certainly quite old. But all of this is not to be compared at all with the uncommonly large number of Freemasons in France. It is well known that fashion is the tyrant in this nation that determines everything. Scarcely had Masonry been established in this kingdom and gained several men of rank as members—those who set the tone in the world—than the Frenchman already considered it an essential requirement of a <term>gallant man<note>In the 18th-century sense, a "gallant man" (galanter Mann) referred to a sophisticated, socially adept gentleman of high standing.</note></term> to be a Freemason. And now the number of members of this order increased to such an extent that one encounters Freemasons there in all classes and ranks of people, from the <term>princes of the blood<note>A prince of the blood (prince du sang) is a person legitimately descended in the male line from a sovereign.</note></term> down to the hairdresser and the servant. the Order increased immensely in lodges, which for the most part followed the English Constitution <note>The "English Constitution" refers to the organizational rules established by the Premier Grand Lodge of England in 1717.</note>. With the war from the years 1757 to 1763 <note>The Seven Years' War.</note>, Masonry <term>Maurerey: Freemasonry</term> spread even more disproportionately throughout Germany, to which the French contributed the most; according to their constitution, an immense number of lodges were established across all of Germany. But nothing surpasses the growth that Masonry in Germany has received since the year 1763, as it became a formal business for many—or rather, for those who set themselves up as "reformers"—to expand Masonry and to flood it with a multitude of members. This was also the point in time when the Order was splintered into various parties, and each of them directed its main focus toward gaining greater prestige through the sheer number of its members. Since these indeed unfortunate times, the number of German Masons has not only equaled the English and French; but if one takes all those who count themselves as German Ma—

To Russia, **Masonry** <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> was brought by the English, French, and Germans, and all of these have established many and numerous **Lodges** <note>original: "Logen"</note> in the various regions of the Empire. These followers, however, are very different from one another, depending on whether they trace their origin to the English, French, or German branches and their various factions. Here too, then, the Order of Freemasons is a **society** <note>original: "Societät"</note> that possesses an uncommonly large following.

I return now to the former point, and freely admit to anyone that nothing contrasts so much with the value of a secret as a great multitude of people. But Masonry, according to its origin, its first foundation, and its essence, was anything but a society that should be numerous and cause a stir in the world. Therefore, if there have been and still are Freemasons who place their worth, their prestige, and their power in the large number of members—who have perhaps even sent out their <term>apostolic emissaries<note>original: "emissarios apostolicos"; a sarcastic comparison to religious missionaries sent to convert others.</note></term> into all the world to convert other Freemasons to their system, and have demanded of their lodges to procure such and such a number of members—then this is a convincing proof that such Masons are not at all informed of the true spirit and purpose of the Order.

--- Page 354 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Masonic Order's organizational structure and geopolitical relevance, following the previous discussion on the nature of secret societies.</meta>

If the Order were something military—if it were intended to one day set out to drive the Grand Turk <note>A common historical term for the Sultan of the Ottoman Empire.</note> out of Europe, and if the Freemasons intended to unexpectedly assist the House of Austria or Russia in this endeavor—then it might be acceptable. But since this is not the case, no further explanation is needed regarding what one should think of such Freemasons. 

Furthermore, it is an established fact that the larger and more widespread a society becomes, the less harmony, firm cohesion, and precise agreement can be achieved within it; the closer it comes to disorder and the inevitable downfall associated with it. This is evident from the history of all times and of all Orders that have ever existed in the world, and is to be expected given the nature of the human heart. But beyond this, those Freemasons who suffer from this "mania for expansion" <term>Erweiterungssucht: a compulsive desire to increase membership or territory</term> provide clear and irrefutable testimony to their own internal weakness and the meager value of their inner secrets.

I see herewith, at the same time, an important objection. ## XVI.
# On Several Recent Writings Concerning Freemasonry.

I could have concluded the present reflections with the preceding section. In that part, everything I know regarding the ancient mysteries <note>The "mysteries" refer to the secret religious rites of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian or Egyptian mysteries, which Freemasons often claimed as their ancestors.</note> has been stated; likewise, what I was able to say regarding the modern mysteries, and what is appropriate to the general state of affairs, has also been expressed and explained therein. However, just then, a few writings from our times—so prolific in Masonic literature—fell into my hands, which I could not allow to remain entirely unused. Whoever has a calling for reviewing <note>original: "recensiren"</note> should, in truth, properly review them now. Nevertheless, it will not be entirely unpleasant for my readers if I say a few words about them. Perhaps I may even accomplish something good through this; and if I [can warn] brothers against Masonic errors...

<sig>X 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>The author introduces a new section intended to review contemporary Masonic literature, bridging his previous historical analysis of ancient mysteries with the practical errors found in modern publications.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, ancient mysteries, literary criticism, Masonic history, Enlightenment publications</keywords>

--- Page 365 ---
<meta>The catchword "ich" (I) from the previous page connects to the opening sentence of this page, continuing the author's defense of his work.</meta>

...of both [friends] and strangers, I still have the merit regarding the Order that I remove incorrect concepts from it which could be detrimental to its reputation.

The first piece of writing bears the title: *On Jesuits, Freemasons, and Rosicrucians*. According to the information provided, it is said to have been published by a former member of the Society of Jesus <note>The Jesuits</note> named Joseph Aloisius Maier. He is identified as the author of the essay on the Jesuits, claims to have received the letter on Masonry from the hands of an old, genuine member of that society, and finally is said to have drawn the essay on today's so-called German Rosicrucians from original documents. I cannot deny that this strange assortment <note>original: "Assortissement"</note> struck me as very alienating from the very beginning, and I still cannot comprehend how Jesuits, Freemasons, and Rosicrucians come together here—groups that otherwise stand so far apart that any cooperation is unthinkable. Misled...

<summary>The author discusses a publication by Joseph Aloisius Maier that attempts to link the Jesuits, Freemasons, and Rosicrucians, expressing skepticism about grouping these disparate organizations together.</summary>
<keywords>Jesuits, Freemasons, Rosicrucians, Joseph Aloisius Maier, secret societies, 18th-century polemics</keywords>

--- Page 366 ---

I, however, do not think so at all; rather, the entire intention of the author has been to deal a fatal blow to the so-called German Rosicrucians <note>The Rosicrucians were a mysterious spiritual and cultural movement that emerged in the early 17th century, often associated with alchemy and esoteric wisdom.</note> and to justify, as much as he is able, that which he regards as Masonry.

Since the author identifies himself as a Jesuit who has been educated in the secret principles of the Society of Jesus <note>The Society of Jesus is a religious order of the Catholic Church; in the 18th century, they were often the subject of conspiracy theories regarding their influence.</note>, one might expect the most significant information from him on that account. This is not only because Jesuits cannot be brought into any sort of agreement with the latter, and because—according to all my convictions regarding the Order <term>Order: referring here to the organized structure of Freemasonry</term>—I still consider it an absolute impossibility that a Jesuit could be a genuine Freemason, or a Freemason be a Jesuit; but primarily because he identifies himself as one who is not himself a Freemason or a Rosicrucian.

But whether they play along, whether there is only one Head Lodge in Germany that stands in connection with the true source—these are all things that are easily said, but could hardly be proven.

It is also far too vague when it is stated: "all Freemasonry is true Freemasonry." (**) If "true" means that in all lodges one is perfectly instructed in the truth of the Order and can be led directly to its purpose, then one can say this of only extremely few; and I doubt very much that the aforementioned single Head Lodge can be counted among that small number. From *Magister Pianco* <note>A pseudonym for Hans Heinrich von Ecker und Eckhoffen (1750–1790), a former member of the Gold and Rosy Cross who published an exposé of the order in 1781.</note>, who has exposed the new Rosicrucians in their nakedness, one would have expected more, as he was a man who himself belonged to this *Society*. I must shed some light on this, as this author has seen fit to venture into a subject that lay too far beyond his reach: namely, the *Mysteries* of the Ancients.

### XVIII.

# On the Disagreements Among the Freemasons.

I come now to shed some light upon a subject which is of great importance to both Masons and strangers, and this concerns the disagreements prevailing among the Freemasons.

One would certainly expect nothing less than that in an Order, which from beginning to end breathes nothing but brotherly love, disagreements should take place. And yet, nothing is more certain than this. As long as one allows impartiality and fairness to speak, this can surprise no one. The Christian Church is the most perfect society that can be imagined. Love is its head and fundamental law: and yet, into how many parties is it splintered, who hate and persecute one another!

<note>The author uses the "Christian Church" as a rhetorical parallel to argue that even the most ideal institutions are subject to human frailty and sectarianism.</note>

<summary>This page introduces a discussion on the internal conflicts within Freemasonry, defending the existence of such disputes by comparing them to the historical schisms within the Christian Church.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, internal conflict, brotherly love, institutional schism, Christian Church analogy</keywords>

--- Page 405 ---
<meta>The text continues the discussion on the imperfections of human institutions, specifically comparing the Masonic Order to ancient mystery schools. Catchword "Gly:" likely refers to Glykon, the serpent god mentioned on the following page.</meta>

For it is established among men, and has men as its members. One can therefore expect nothing more perfect from the **Masonic Order** <note>original: "Maurerorden"</note>, which is not founded outside of this world and likewise draws its members from among humanity. In a society as widespread and numerous as **Masonry** <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> is, one could hardly expect anything else. The sentiments, concepts, and intentions of men are too diverse; it is therefore no wonder that these differences eventually degenerate into disagreements within a large society—especially one that lacks the external means to keep such differences within proper bounds.

Just as little was the prestige of the true **mysteries** <note>The secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries</note> among the ancients diminished if, on occasion, duty-forgetful citizens made use of the sacred darkness and the silence of the mysteries to hide their harmful political plans beneath them; or if a "prophet" **Alexander** <note>Referring to Alexander of Abonoteichos, a 2nd-century false prophet who established a cult around a serpent-god</note> with his new god

<summary>The author argues that Freemasonry, like all human institutions and ancient mysteries, is naturally subject to internal disagreement and occasional misuse due to human imperfection.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, human nature, ancient mysteries, Alexander of Abonoteichos, institutional integrity, secrecy</keywords>

# Page 369

Just as the false prophet Glykon <note>A reference to the "Serpent God" hoax perpetrated by Alexander of Abonoteichos in the 2nd century AD</note> deceived the simple-minded commoners of Paphlagonia <note>An ancient region on the northern coast of Anatolia</note>; just as little can it bring the slightest disadvantage to true Masonry <term>Maurerey: Freemasonry</term> in the eyes of fair and thinking men when they hear of such separations and divisions among the Masons. This matter, however, deserves a closer investigation.

Not very long after the founding of Masonry, such differences already took place to a certain extent. This arose partly because those among whom Masonry first originated were not inclined to communicate it to others; and when it nevertheless became somewhat known to others, these people sought to replace that which they lacked in another way. Partly, however, these differences owed their origin to the different way of thinking <term>Denkungsart: mindset or philosophy</term> of the peoples among whom Masonry was established. Despite all these differences, however, for entire centuries one knew of no discord, of no separation among the Masons. One spoke of full—

<meta>The previous page discussed the initial obligations and unity of the Order; this page continues by describing the historical shift that led to internal divisions.</meta>

to which they had bound themselves upon their first entry into the Order <note>The author refers here to the "Order" of Freemasonry</note>. This was also the best path that could ever have been chosen for such a widespread Society.

But approximately 17 to 18 years ago, this changed. A significant revolution occurred within Masonry in Germany, and from this actually arose the disagreements, sects, and systems which, from that time onward, have most miserably splintered this harmonious-brotherly Order. I firmly believe that the desire of some German Freemasons to be ever better and more deeply instructed in the secrets <note>original: "Geheimnissen"</note> of the Order provided the first occasion for this. However, that would have accomplished little or nothing at all, had there not been people who believed they could use this inclination of the Freemasons to their own advantage: for even centuries before, the Order had never lacked members who were said to have sought to be ever better and more perfectly instructed.

<summary>The author describes a "revolution" in German Freemasonry occurring around 1760-1770 that fractured the Order's unity into competing sects and systems due to a misplaced desire for deeper secrets.</summary>

--- Page 409 ---
But now, people truly stood up who took advantage of this curiosity, presenting their system—as they called it—as the only true Freemasonry. They gained a following not only through the deceptive claim that they had strictly maintained the observance of the Order <note>The "Order" refers to the Freemasons; "observance" likely refers to the "Strict Observance," a specific high-degree system of Masonry popular in the 18th century</note>, which had elsewhere been corrupted, and sought to bind this following to themselves all the more firmly through all sorts of glittering hopes and false pretenses; they also strove to enlarge their numbers through their missionaries, who were supposedly sent to reform the corrupted Order. Informed people know of the changes <unclear>that occurred</unclear>. Even to attentive strangers, this has not entirely escaped notice.

Here arose the great schism in the Masonic Order, which will perhaps never be healed. For these alleged reformers, who wished to trample underfoot everything that did not enlist under their banner, declared all other Freemasons to be illegitimate <note>original: "unächt"</note>, and closed <unclear>their doors</unclear>

<meta>The previous page discussed the initial tensions and the emergence of new leadership within the secret societies. This page continues the narrative of the formal schism and the resulting animosity between the factions.</meta>

their lodges, forbade their members from visiting the lodges of their former friends, and even from accepting letters from them concerning the Order; or at the very least, they were required to send such letters to the leaders of the new Reformation <note>The "Reformation" refers to the Strict Observance movement, a 18th-century reform of Freemasonry that introduced a hierarchical, quasi-military structure</note> and to draft their replies according to the instructions of the same. It is easy to understand that this kind of excommunication <note>original: "Excommunication" - used here metaphorically to describe the formal expulsion or shunning of members from a fraternal body</note> produced a similar reaction on the other side. Even if one did not proceed with such despotic severity, the schism was nonetheless established: people named themselves according to this or that system or observance <term>Observance: a specific branch or "rite" of Freemasonry following a particular set of rules and historical claims</term>. And as there has never been a schism in the world where hatred and discord did not intermingle, so it happened here as well. Among those who had previously constituted a single universal society whose members loved one another, there now arose two main parties who mutually hated each other.

But this was, so to speak, only the beginning. For however firmly those who were the authors of that unfortunate separation among brothers...

From 15 to 17 years ago, the only **masonic body** <note>original: "maurerische Körper"; referring to the unified structure of the fraternity</note> which for entire centuries had constituted a single brotherly whole, has been miserably torn into various parties.

One would be greatly mistaken to believe that these differences concern mere trifles. To be sure, those very individuals who were the authors of the divisions within the **Order** <note>The "Order" refers to the organized system of Freemasonry</note> have often focused their attention on such trivialities. This is no wonder. Have not **theologians** and **grammarians** separated themselves from their brothers over a single expression, a distinction, or a construction? And have not monks miserably persecuted one another over whether a hood should be long, pointed, or round? Why then should **Freemasons** not also be able to quarrel over such things, especially when they boast of having maintained exactly and strictly that which has been well-established since antiquity? But these disagreements and differences nevertheless always concern matters of importance, which are nothing less—

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the general structure or history of the Order; this page begins a specific classification of its internal factions. Catchword "Ei" likely refers to "Eifer" (zeal) or "Eitelkeit" (vanity) appearing on the following page.</meta>

...as concerns the purpose and the internal object of the Masonic Order <note>original: "Maurer-Ordens"</note>, and in this respect, one can actually assume three main parties. The first has directed its primary attention toward things which, if one examines them closely, are not worthy of being occupied with. This group either thinks of no secrets at all, or to a certain extent gives them up, and in their place sets plans that, fundamentally examined, are chimeras <note>An illusion or a fabrication of the mind; a goal that is impossible to achieve.</note>, even if one does not go so far as to say that they run entirely contrary to the first fundamental rules of the Order. This is their object, and the purpose toward which they hasten. One perceives, without my reminding, that this party is a waste of time and money for everyone; and it is also characteristic of this party to suppress everything that will not agree with it. For however vain their plans are, they pursue them just as zealously, and try now in this way, now in another, to achieve their intentions.

<summary>This page describes the first of three factions within the Masonic Order, characterizing it as a group focused on vain, illusory goals that waste resources and suppress dissenting views.</summary>
<keywords>Masonic Order, secret societies, internal factions, chimeras, organizational criticism, Enlightenment era</keywords>

Another party <note>The author uses "party" to mean a faction or school of thought within the secret society.</note> rejects both: that object as well as that purpose. They laugh just as much at those plans as they disapprove of their entire conduct. They do the Order the justice of acknowledging that the most sublime secrets lie hidden within its bosom. They seek to track these secrets down. However, they exaggerate; they form ideas that have no basis and choose means that are not the right ones. Where the former see no secrets at all, but only their society and their plans, these people see nothing but extraordinary things. Lacking the ability to distinguish truth from fiction, they often fall into the hands of people who exploit their thirst for mystery to use them, leading them from one labyrinth into another. I believe I am not mistaken if I place the so-called **Rosicrucianism** <note>A spiritual and cultural movement that arose in the early 17th century, claiming to possess esoteric wisdom from the ancient past.</note> on this side. — The third party, in a certain sense, belongs to neither of the two. They admit the secrets of the Order, and they believe, and

it is rightly said that they are the concern of only a few men. This group neither devises grand schemes nor goes hunting for hidden secrets. It seeks to form its members into good, loving, and civilized human beings, and believes that by doing so, it fulfills the entire purpose of the Order. Many French and English lodges are of this kind, as are many among the Germans. They are certainly not free of faults; however, they are always far safer for the stranger than those other two systems. If a Mason of perfect knowledge <note>original: "Erkenntnis"; referring here to deep spiritual or philosophical insight</note> wished to select a student for himself, he would have to choose his pupil from among these Masons, whose heads are not yet filled with overstrained concepts and whose hearts are not yet devastated by foolish imaginings.

The true Mason <term>Mason: a member of the Freemasons, a fraternal organization focused on moral and intellectual improvement</term> looks past all these parties. He knows the unfortunate circumstances to which they owe their existence. He recognizes them all as Masons and as brothers, even though he may find little to please him in their works, their
